#249. Starbucks sales miss expectations, but its U.S. turnaround is gaining steam

Starbucks on Thursday reported mixed quarterly results as its international cafes underperformed expectations.

However, its U.S. turnaround continues to take hold, as its home market topped expectations during the coffee giant's final quarter of fiscal 2026.

"We enter fiscal 2027 with momentum, a clear set of priorities and higher ambitions for what Starbucks can become," CEO Brian Niccol said in a video shared on the company's website.

Starbucks is ahead of schedule to meet the fiscal 2028 targets laid out at its investor day in January, he said on the company's earnings conference call.

For fiscal 2027, Starbucks is projecting adjusted earnings per share in a range of $3.15 to $3.25, above Wall Street's expectations of earnings per share of $3.14. However, its anticipated revenue decline of 1% is weaker than expected; analysts were forecasting net sales growth of 1.4%.

Starbucks is projecting North American same-store sales growth of 4% for the fiscal year.

The company's earnings and revenue outlook includes an additional week and the effects of the shift in its China business to a joint venture business model. Starbucks is also projecting slight deflation in coffee prices and slowing net new North American cafe openings, which will both weigh on its full-year revenue, according to CFO Cathy Smith.

Here's what the company reported for the quarter ended Sept. 27 compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Starbucks reported fiscal fourth-quarter net income attributable to the company of $677.2 million, or 59 cents per share, up from $133.1 million, or 12 cents per share, a year earlier.

Excluding restructuring and impairment costs and other items, the company reported earnings per share of 80 cents.

Net revenue fell 4% to $9.14 billion, reflecting the joint venture deal in China.

The company's global same-store sales increased 7.2%, fueled by growth in average ticket, and beating Wall Street estimates of 6%, according to StreetAccount. Transactions grew 1.8% in the quarter.

Starbucks' U.S. cafes reported same-store sales growth of 7.7%, topping StreetAccount estimates of a 6.5% increase.

Under Niccol's "Back to Starbucks" strategy, the company has focused on improving service, making its cafes more welcoming and sharpening its marketing. The changes have helped to win back customers.

"Starbucks brand affinity is at near record levels as our menu innovation, marketing and merchandise continue to create moments that drive culture and bring people into our coffee houses," Niccol told analysts.

The company said its North American rollout of its fall menu, which includes the iconic pumpkin spice latte, was the company's strongest ever autumn launch day. And matcha is now a $1 billion drink for Starbucks.

Outside of North America, the company's international comparable sales ticked up 0.9%, as traffic declines offset an increase to average ticket. That growth fell short of analyst estimates of a 3.2% increase, according to StreetAccount.

After its China business transitioned to a joint venture, Starbucks has shifted most of its international business to a licensed model, allowing local operators to take charge. The company is also exploring selling a majority stake in its Japan business, Reuters reported in September.

Starbucks opened 303 net new cafes during the quarter. In September, the company announced it would close about 1% of its North American footprint, or about 250 locations, as part of its turnaround.

By the end of its fiscal year, across the U.S. and Canada, the company also completed more than 1,700 cafe uplifts, meant to make its stores more welcoming to customers. The company has said locations that receive a makeover, with tweaks like increased seating and softer lighting, see higher transactions in the aftermath.

#248. Europe's car makers are in crisis. Will the threat of war rescue them?

Europe's car makers are in crisis. Will the threat of war rescue them?

It's green, bulging and intimidating. Ford's latest vehicle looks like a pick-up truck that has been through an Incredible Hulk transformation. Based on its popular Ranger series, this beefy number parked outside the front door of Ford's Dagenham plant can carry a load of two tonnes, tow up to four tonnes and is also shouldering the hopes of the 2,000-strong workforce that still makes engines here.

Inside the factory, the three-litre diesel engines that will power this camo-painted beast drift down a production line that has seen output cut from 90,000 engines a year to about half that over the last decade.

Ford is hoping that a pivot towards military vehicles can help fill the void created by what the company's UK boss calls the most challenging environment since the invention of the motor car. Once mighty, the European car industry is starting to look puny and is hoping surging defence budgets as Europe re-arms can help it flex its industrial muscles once more.

Ford is part of a bid for the British Army's Light Mobility Vehicle (LMV) programme

Ford is part of a joint venture with defence specialists General Dynamics and Ricardo bidding for a Ministry of Defence (MoD) contract to supply 9,000 vehicles over the next five to seven years to replace the Army's ageing Land Rover-based fleet.

Lisa Brankin, chair of Ford UK, says it is a chance to showcase their abilities to respond quickly to defence needs: "As a manufacturer you look at every opportunity that comes at you and this is a great opportunity that we would love to take advantage of."

This is not the first time Ford has busied itself with defence work.

Ahead of World War Two, Ford's factory in Dagenham, east London, was the largest car plant in Europe.

When war broke out, civilian car assembly stopped completely, and the factory was converted entirely to military production. Between 1939 and 1945, the Dagenham plant built 360,000 vehicles for the Allied war effort.

Ford workers in Manchester manufactured 34,000 Rolls-Royce-designed Merlin engines, which powered Spitfires and Hurricane fighter planes.

Eighty years later, the UK and European car industries are hoping that the engines of war can help defend against what one supplier told the BBC was a "terminal decline".

As Europe feels compelled to commit hundreds of billions to ramp up defence spending in the face of the menace from Russia and US reluctance to be Europe's protector, car makers that are under commercial attack from Chinese rivals have taken notice.

So can the rearmament of Europe save an auto industry and a supply chain facing crisis?

Ford is far from the only car company that sees defence as a growth industry - and one that can utilise the growing overcapacity at car plants across Europe.

French car maker Renault has signed a strategic agreement with defence giant Thales to produce military drones - targeting an output of up to 1,000 units per month. The French military and Directorate General for Armament want to tap into Renault's mass-production capabilities to bypass traditional, slower defence supply chains.

Meanwhile, Volkswagen has agreed to sell an under-used factory in Osnabruck, western Germany, which will become a military manufacturing hub in a joint venture with an Israeli-based defence investor.

Jaguar Land Rover (JLR), which makes the Land Rover, is also bidding for the same contract as Ford - as the Army retires its existing Land Rover-based fleet by 2030. JLR has also established a new dedicated business unit to support its global military ambitions.

It makes sense for under-utilised auto manufacturing capacity to switch to defence, says Mike Hawes of the Society for Motor Manufacturers and Traders (SMMT) trade association.

The UK car industry and its suppliers are heavily reliant on a few big manufacturers â Nissan in Sunderland, Toyota in Derbyshire, BMW in Oxfordshire and the biggest of them all by value of output, JLR at multiple sites in the Midlands and Merseyside.

And Hawes says the supply chain is very vulnerable.

"UK automotive output has been in decline over the last eight or nine years," he says. "We're probably half of what we were 10 years ago. Now, that's obviously going to hit the supply chain because they're not making the same number of parts. So they've got capacity. They may be quite dependent on one particular manufacturer as well."

So they will welcome "the opportunity to broaden their customer base and potentially move into defence", he says.

Just two weeks ago, JLR announced it was cutting 4,000 jobs from its 30,000-strong UK workforce in order to reduce costs to stay competitive with international â particularly Chinese â rivals. Dave Roberts of the firm Evtec, which supplies cooling systems components to JLR, is worried about the implications of this.

"JLR is the critical mass in the UK automotive manufacturing space," he says. "It is the glue that holds the whole of the sector together. Because remember, when you're making volumes for JLR, they're significantly higher than any other car maker in the UK.

"If they suffer, the ripples run deeper through the supply chain."

Earlier this week, major JLR suppliers urged the government to help automotive manufacturers move into aerospace and defence, warning that large-scale car production in the UK faces long-term decline.

Executives have warned of a "visible crack" in the UK automotive supply chain

In an open letter to the prime minister, chancellor and West Midlands Mayor Richard Parker, industry leaders argued Britain's automotive supply chain was "not in decline" but "in the wrong market".

The letter was signed by executives representing businesses with more than 8,600 direct employees, alongside the Confederation of British Metalforming, which represents about 75,000 workers.

Signatories to the letter said the redundancies were "the first visible crack" in a UK automotive supply chain supporting about 183,000 manufacturing jobs.

Across Europe, the industry is facing what Sigrid de Vries, director general of the ACEA auto industry manufacturers' association called "a perfect storm". Car makers are spending billions to shift to electric vehicles and insist that government sales targets for EVs are outpacing consumer demand.

And the EVs people are buying are increasingly from the biggest new threat to the industry â China.

In the early 2000s, Western firms saw China as a land of opportunity. The country's rapidly growing middle class had plenty of money and a seemingly insatiable appetite for cars, including the most profitable upmarket models. It was a lucrative business â the Chinese market once accounted for half of Volkswagen's profits.

However, it didn't last. China wanted a home-grown car industry and lavish state funding was ploughed into making the country a leader in high-tech businesses, including electric cars.

Today, the Chinese market is overloaded with brands, both foreign and domestic, and is characterised by cut-throat competition. Chinese car makers have focused their attention on foreign markets, and have used the transition to electric vehicles as a pathway to gaining market share. The likes of BYD, Chery and Geely are moving aggressively into Europe.

For European brands, all of this could not have happened at a worse time. The loss of the steady stream of profits from China, and the emergence of Chinese rivals on their home turf, has come after they invested heavily in producing electric vehicles (EVs). But EV sales have not increased as quickly as expected. Executives admit they have struggled to match the low production costs and development speed of the Chinese insurgents.

The result is that European manufacturers are now rushing to cut costs, while wondering what to do with expensive factories capable of producing millions more cars than they are able to sell.

Volkswagen has already announced plans to cut 100,000 jobs over the next few years. Whereas once closing plants in Germany would have been unthinkable, the company has now shuttered one in Dresden and may shut down four more. That includes a site in Zwickau, where VW spent over â¬1bn (Â£857m) converting production lines to build electric vehicles - a process that was completed just four years ago.

Industry estimates indicate that western European car plants have roughly 2.5 million vehicles worth of annual spare capacity.

Little wonder that car makers are jealously eyeing soaring defence budgets across Europe.

Sigrid de Vries says car makers are well placed to help Europe rearm.

"Many of the capabilities that defence needs are needed for and also delivered by the automotive sector," she says. "So automotive manufacturers and suppliers possess industrial assets, they possess manufacturing expertise, logistics capabilities, also advanced technologies. They have vast and also very integrated supply chains that may be relevant for Europe's broader defence preparedness objective."

But it is not that simple â security protocols, political and economic rivalries within Europe plus the fact that unless you're actually at war, the volumes will not replace the mass consumer market, all of which present challenges, she says.

"These are two very different worlds," she says. Governments want to invest in their defence capabilities and that is why it's now, more than ever, interesting for manufacturers and suppliers to see what's possible, but it will not be enough to address the underutilisation of manufacturing capacity we currently see."

If defence can't plug the yawning gaps in UK and European car manufacturing production â is it time for the henhouse to rent a room to the fox?

In other words, let Chinese companies move into European production facilities?

To offset the immense fixed costs of running idle assembly lines, legacy European and UK automakers are opening their doors to Chinese rivals.

Stellantis â owners of the Vauxhall, Fiat, Peugeot and Citroen marques â has taken a 20% stake in Chinese EV maker Leapmotor and production of the Chinese brand started in Poland two years ago. (Leapmotor then moved production to Spain after Poland voted to impose steep tariffs on Chinese EVs while Spain abstained â an example of how trade politics can get complicated.)

Nissan and Chery International UK have signed a non-binding Memorandum of Understanding for the study of contract manufacturing for Nissan to manufacture Chery vehicles at its Sunderland plant.

VW CEO Oliver Blume said in April of this year that Volkswagen was considering sharing spare European factory capacity with Chinese joint-venture partners.

In one sense it could be a win-win. European factories would have extra work while Chinese manufacturers could avoid high tariffs when selling into the US and the EU by making and exporting the cars in and from those markets.

But building cars in Europe does not automatically mean creating or saving European supply chains.

Some plants may only perform final assembly while many of the components â especially batteries â may continue to come from China.

Cultural treasures are being destroyed worldwide - so why is no-one being punished?

War may be coming. Are we psychologically ready?

Why a fresh wave of North Korean soldiers may fight in Russia's war in Ukraine

Car making is still seen as central to countries' sovereign manufacturing capability â in the old days the mantra was: "What's good for General Motors is good for America." You could say the same for Volkswagen, Mercedes and BMW for Germany and JLR for the UK.

Car plants are often the major employer in their area and support local supply chains, so job losses can hit particularly hard. That's why countries go to great lengths to protect and preserve them.

Australia is a case in point. When its final locally made car left the production line in 2017, it didn't just lose a car industry, it lost an engineering mindset and key skills, says Dave Roberts of Evtec.

"Over the next decade... ripple effects crept into all manufacturing," he says. "So they lost infrastructure capability. They lost advanced manufacturing capability.

"They're vulnerable. They're not resilient in those sectors anymore."

Ford UK's Lisa Brankin acknowledges 9,000 engines over five to seven years is a far cry from the 90,000 per year the plant used to produce â but she insists every little helps.

"It is a drop in the ocean but every single opportunity is worth having, isn't it?"

A spokesperson for the Ministry of Defence says it wants UK industry to play a "central role" in delivering thousands of modern light mobility vehicles. The spokesperson adds: "We are backing British businesses and supporting our defence industrial base with 85% of our defence spending currently staying in the UK, driving reindustrialisation and making defence an engine for growth."

It's understandable why a declining European car industry wants to gatecrash the spending splurge in the defence sector. But even if they can, right now it is hard to see how they'll ever be able to wield the muscle that they once did.

Correction 25 September: A graph that incorrectly implied there was a target for European defence spending to rise to â¬800bn by 2030 has been removed from this piece. It has been replaced by a line chart showing EU defence spending, both actual and projected, from 2018 to 2029.

#246. Blood banks targeted in latest European fridge cyberattacks

French blood banks were on Monday affected by the recent wave of cybersecurity attacks on European refrigeration systems, the French Blood Establishment confirmed on Wednesday.

The organization said that while it wasn't the direct target of the attack, cold storage units operated by external providers containing the EFS' blood products were compromised across three of its sites, with hackers cranking up temperatures.

"Specifically, temperature and defrost thresholds were set to excessively high levels," the EFS said in a statement translated from French to English by CNBC.

"These cold storage units—located at EFS preparation and distribution sites—are used to store blood products prior to their delivery to healthcare facilities, as well as samples intended for EFS biobanks."

They also stored frozen plasma used for transfusions the manufacturing of medicinal products.

The EFS said that its surveillance systems were triggered rapidly, and depending on the situation, affected equipment was either brought under control with no impact on the products or samples, or they were taken offline. No samples or blood products were lost during the attack, with samples quickly transferred to other facilities, the EFS added.

The EFS is now coordinating with the French National Cybersecurity Agency (ANSSI) and the Ministry of Health and has increased monitoring of its equipment

Hundreds of supermarkets across France and other European countries, including Belgium, Sweden and Finland, had their fridges remotely hacked and temperatures raised, resulting in potentially millions of euros of spoiled food.

"Several regional facilities of the French Blood ​Establishment (EFS) were affected by a cybersecurity incident ​that impacted certain cold storage room monitoring and control ‌systems,"

The situation is now under control and is being closely monitored, the French Health Ministry said in a statement, according to Reuters. They added that the safety of the blood supplies was ​preserved "thanks ​to the ⁠facilities' monitoring and alarm systems, as well as precautionary measures immediately ​implemented."

#245. My hometown shows that high streets have to change or die

My hometown shows that high streets have to change or die

The empty building that used to be the home of Marks & Spencer in Aberdeen is one of the first things you see when you arrive off the train and come into the city centre. The sign has gone, though the shape of the lettering can still be made out; the windows are blacked out and the doors boarded up.

This is my hometown. As a teenager, I spent hours on Union Street, its main thoroughfare, nicknamed the Granite Mile after the local silver-grey stone. It was the place to try on all the latest fashions from the likes of Clockhouse and Chelsea Girl.

The street used to be packed with shoppers from across the north-east of Scotland.

"I come to Union Street to take a bus somewhere else... there's nothing to shop for nowâ¦ it's horrendous," complains Marie Sim, a lifelong Aberdeen resident.

It's a similar story across the country: High Streets, once bursting with shops, hit first by the rise of out-of-town retail centres and then by online commerce.

But Aberdeen is in the thick of an ambitious turnaround plan. The port city has essentially declared war on its empty shop problem, taking an unusually hands-on approach to filling vacant units and bringing long-neglected upper floors of buildings into use.

The Marks & Spencer flagship store in Aberdeen closed in August, 80 years after it opened there

On top of that, the council is pouring tens of millions into trying to make the city centre a destination again. Like many other places, it's leaning into the "experience economy", with a new indoor food and drink market and a major redevelopment of the seafront.

High Streets are integral to how we feel about where we live. The sight of vacant shops and graffiti can quickly become a vicious circle, deterring shoppers, discouraging investment and leading to anti-social behaviour.

Politicians of all stripes have been trying for years to breathe new life into town centres - and Andy Burnham, who has made a focus on "place" part of his political identity, is the latest. He argues they should become "symbols of Britain's renaissance".

But can it be achieved? And if they're to survive, do town centres need a fundamental rethink?

One in eight properties on British High Streets are currently vacant, according to Green Street, the property and retail analytics company, a number that's stayed stubbornly high for years.

"If there was a silver bullet, it would have been fired years ago," says Jackie Sadek, a former government adviser on urban regeneration, who has spent nearly four decades working in town centre renewal.

One of the biggest obstacles is fractured ownership. Shops and buildings are often owned by dozens of different landlords, sometimes through offshore companies or complex structures, each with their own interests and priorities. That can slow decision-making and make large-scale regeneration difficult.

But even with empty units that are available, you need to find someone willing to take them on.

One in eight properties on British High Streets are currently vacant

The cost of running a High Street business has also risen; employment costs are higher, and there's the burden of business rates.

Firms need customers. Footfall on many High Streets has been in long-term decline as shopping and services move online, and this enormous structural shift has yet to play out fully.

Two-thirds of town centre experts think there is still 20% to 40% too much retail space, according to a survey by retail body Revo and real estate consultancy Lambert Smith Hampton.

In Aberdeen's boom years of North Sea oil, from the late 1970s through to the early 2010s, it didn't have to work hard to fill its hotels and restaurants and get the cash tills ringing because of the wealth the energy industry brought to the city.

I remember weekends with friends as a teenager, spending whatever cash we had in the fashion chains and shoe shops. Later came the pubs and bar-hopping, and trips to the art deco Capitol Theatre (now office space), where I saw Duran Duran.

But by the end of 2022, Union Street had hit rock bottom. Aberdonians had long deserted the High Street for a shopping centre, which opened in 2009 next to the railway station.

"They'd fallen out of love with their city," says Bob Keiller, head of Our Union Street coalition, a community-led group formed after an emergency summit to reverse the decline.

The first thing it did was listen to what residents wanted, receiving thousands of submissions. Restoring a bit of civic pride and filling empty units became the focus.

It's been a co-ordinated effort, involving letting agents, the council, landlords, businesses and a big team of volunteers, led by Keiller, a former FTSE 100 CEO, who isn't getting paid. He has a map on his office wall, detailing the status of every property, unit by unit, with colour-coded Post-it notes.

They audited the street, tracking down absent landlords, and logging what was empty and why. Volunteers offered to give the empty properties a clean and a lick of paint, to make them more attractive to let. They also spruced up the streets, picking litter, weeding, and painting bins. A small team still goes out once a fortnight.

Bob Keiller heads a community-led group set up to help revive Aberdeen's Union Street

There's a website where all the available properties are collated; crucially, all of the financial incentives are also listed. These can include council fit-out grants (where a new business gets money to decorate premises), and a two-year discount from business rates.

In 2023, 57 of the 198 units on Union Street were empty. As of today, 33 have been filled, with a further 14 in the process of being let.

But it can feel like a game of whack-a-mole because since the initiative started, another 20 units have become vacant. "The key thing is, we're filling quicker than emptying," Keiller insists.

Keiller's operations director, David McKeith, describes it as putting an arm round the shoulder of those they can help and being a "wasp in the ear" of people who just say no.

At one end of the office, a small, young team produces social media content, linking visitors and residents to a new Our Aberdeen app, showing what the city can offer.

For Keiller, the "small wins" - like tackling graffiti - are needed to help change public perception.

"We've realised the psychology is not to do it once but be prepared to do it two or three times before people that do these things eventually say, 'there's no point in putting it there because somebody's going to paint over it.'"

Attention is also turning to the often-forgotten upper floors of buildings along the once-iconic Union Street. In a separate initiative run by the business group Aberdeen Inspired, experts are getting inside those vacant high-up spaces to identify the barriers to bringing them back into use.

Aberdeen sought the help of a High Street troubleshooter, Iain Nicholson, who's worked with dozens of towns and cities through his Vacant Shops Academy.

Out of more than 1,000 empty units he helped audit nationally, on average more than half didn't have a letting agent actively marketing the property. He says some councils don't even know who the landlords are.

Empty shops, he says, won't all be filled by the market alone. Towns and cities need to encourage a broader range of occupiers, from arts and culture to leisure, health and well-being.

He says what's striking about Aberdeen is its joined-up approach, and the fact people "rolled up their sleeves" and overcame differences. "That's not something you can yet say about everywhere."

Every town and city faces its own mix of challenges.

Nicholson also helped Crewe in Cheshire. As a smaller place than Aberdeen, it is focusing on arts, culture, and community, with a new women's centre opened this month.

And English councils are being encouraged to take a more interventionist role.

They can now force an empty commercial unit that's been vacant for at least a year to be offered to tenants through a High Street Rental Auction, even if the landlord doesn't want to let it.

A noodle bar (left) is one of the new businesses in the area. Parts of the city centre have been undergoing construction work (right)

It's early days but the mere prospect of losing control of a property is delivering some results. Bassetlaw Council in Nottinghamshire, an early adopter of the scheme, says more than 30 shops across the district have been let or are currently under offer as a result of the policy.

But filling empty units is only part of the challenge. The bigger question is what town and city centres are actually for.

Changing habits mean we won't need so many shops. Instead, it's argued, town centres will need more people living and working in them, with new homes and office space alongside services that can't be done online. With an ageing population, for example, bringing NHS health centres into the heart of a town could be a big opportunity.

In Aberdeen, the building that used to house BHS - a department store shuttered in 2016 - is being converted into an entrance plaza for the new food and drink market.

The physical appearance of Union Street is changing too. Like many Victorian shopping thoroughfares, it was built with a wide road (probably to give space to horses and carriages). Now parts of the pavements are being widened to make it a more pleasant place to spend time, though many Aberdonians are weary of the disruption, and of new traffic restrictions.

Money is a big barrier. Aberdeen's new indoor food and drink market is costing Â£40m, funded by the council and the UK Levelling Up Fund. In other places, regeneration schemes can struggle because the cost of redevelopment outweighs the likely financial return, making support from the public sector essential. And the economics are becoming more challenging.

"Higher interest rates, rising costs of construction and other issues are impacting the viability and delivery of new housing and big redevelopment projects," says Steve Norris, a director at Lambert Smith Hampton. He says that big projects usually require cash from both the public and private sectors.

He wants to see "more creative ways of funding".

Part of Burnham's plan is to give local leaders more freedom to invest in town centres and drive regeneration, building on the approach he has championed in Greater Manchester. But bigger projects take years.

Volunteers have played a role in the redevelopment of Aberdeen's Union Street

Making it easier and cheaper to open and run a High Street business is one of the more immediate solutions being called for, with business rates a particular concern.

Burnham has promised further rates reform. All eyes are on this month's Budget to see how far he will go. All eyes are on this month's Budget to see how far he will go in England. Business rates are devolved in Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland.

The chief executive of Next, Simon Wolfson, argues that the bigger challenge is not business rates, but planning reform.

"The most important thing that the government can do for high streets is let them develop," says Wolfson, who's also a Conservative peer.

Back in Aberdeen, there are some signs of traction.

Lauren Reid and her mother Rose moved their fashion boutique to Union Street in 2024. A council grant helped with the refurbishment after they decided to make the leap.

Can they make the sums add up? "We're getting there," she says.

She feels the same about the future of her street. "I always say Rome wasn't built in a day."

Lauren Reid and her mother Rose moved their fashion boutique to Union Street with the help of a council grant

Right now the area feels like a construction site - but another gaping hole will soon be filled. Robert Keane is a local property developer who in 2023 bought the Trinity shopping centre, once home to Debenhams. The space is now set to be re-occupied by a national leisure brand. A notoriously tiring stairway leading to the Trinity from the train station is also being replaced by an escalator.

"We saw a real good opportunity, because it was dying," he says.

He reckons he can make a go of it, cutting the cost of parking and overheads, resetting rents and being more nimble than previous far-flung owners.

It was a bold move given Lambert Smith Hampton suggests that more than one in 10 UK shopping centres is ripe for demolition.

But he's betting the dynamics of Aberdeen are finally changing.

"I think by 2027 it should look and feel like a different city."

That is certainly the hope. Yet walking along Union Street today, past the boarded-up shopfronts and roadworks, it can still feel like a city caught between its past and whatever comes next.

How the High Street became a window on our political instability

Why the UK is dithering over what to do about e-scooters

The sale of illegal cigarettes signals a deeper problem with UK high streets

#243. Cheaper cars are selling considerably faster than at the beginning of the year

Consumers are concerned about rising fuel prices and affordability — and that could be showing up in how fast the least expensive cars are moving off dealer lots.

From the first quarter to the third, the number of days it took a dealer to sell a vehicle priced between $20,000 and $29,999 dropped 20%, according to data from Edmunds. That pace of selling saw the biggest acceleration of any price segment.

Vehicles priced from $30,000 to $39,999 sold about 18% faster, and the number of days it took to sell the next tier — $40,000 to $49,999 — dropped nearly 12%, according to Edmunds.

All of those price segments also took fewer days to turn than their more expensive counterparts. The lower-priced tiers took under 60 days to move off lots, with the least expensive ones selling the fastest. Every segment of vehicles above $50,000, meanwhile, took more than 60 days to sell.

"If you're a dealer and you have inventory and that price point, you're definitely noticing," said Edmunds Director of Insights Ivan Drury.

Cheaper vehicles may be selling faster, but they're still hard to find. The average listing price for a new vehicle in September was $49,726, according to Cox Automotive.

And, the overall sales numbers for some key segments priced under $40,000 have fallen. This has especially been true for compact cars, which are down roughly 4% since the beginning of 2026, and compact SUVs, down nearly 8% in the same period, according to Cox Automotive.

These different data points seem to be moving in opposite directions. On the one hand, cheaper cars are moving faster off dealer lots. On the other, sales of affordable segments are down.

There are a few reasons for this, according to analysts who follow the industry. One is that there might not be enough lower-priced vehicles on dealer lots in the first place.

"I think there are some supply things going on at that end of the market," said Cox Automotive Deputy Chief Economist Mark Strand. "We have to kind of grade the market on a curve until we see what's going on with fourth-quarter data."

The compact SUV segment, in particular, is suffering from the lack of two popular models.

The Toyota RAV4, which is the most popular SUV in the U.S., has been in short supply at many dealerships this year, as the automaker shifts production to its Kentucky factory, Strand said. The company also has shifted the entire lineup to a hybrid powertrain at a time when hybrids have been in exceptionally high demand.

The other missing SUV is the Ford Escape, which that automaker discontinued to make way for its upcoming midsize EV pickup truck.

The other slow-selling, low-priced segment — compact cars — faces structural inventory challenges, said AutoForecast Solutions Vice President of Global Forecasting Sam Fiorani.

"We've seen the disappearance of affordable, compact new vehicles over the last decade," he said.

Detroit automakers swapped out their lineups of compact coupes and sedans in favor of small crossover SUVs, which tend to carry slightly higher sticker prices than comparably sized passenger cars.

Currently, there is not a single compact passenger sedan for sale by an American manufacturer. Most on the market are Japanese or Korean, with a few German models, such as the Volkswagen Jetta and the Golf hatchback.

"You have all these brands that have just abandoned the marketplace," Fiorani said.

The smallest American passenger cars are the Ford Mustang, General Motors' Chevrolet Corvette, and the midsize Tesla Model 3 sedan, according to AutoForecast Solutions. The firm classifies both the Mustang and the Corvette as sports cars, and as such they occupy a different niche than compact sedans like the Honda Civic or Toyota Corolla.

The Mustang has a starting price of $33,490, the Model 3 starts at $34,990 and the Chevrolet Corvette starts at $71,300. Cars like the Civic, Corolla and Jetta, meanwhile, all start below $25,000.

"Compact vehicle buyers are very price-sensitive," Fiorani said. "And so when the price of gas goes up, that eats into their car payment. That means that they can't afford a $500 or $600 a month car payment, and they have to move into the used market."

Indeed, the wholesale used prices of compact cars have risen and are up more than almost any other segment, according to Cox Automotive. Wholesale prices — the prices vehicles sell for at large auctions, like Cox Automotive's Manheim auction — are indicators for prices overall in the fragmented used-car market.

Along with the rise in prices, auto finance stats show that a historically high number of buyers are stretching to afford cars.

Roughly one in four buyers financing their purchases are getting 84-month loans — a record. Nearly 3 in 10 trade-ins toward new-vehicle purchases carried negative equity in the third quarter, meaning the cars being traded in were worth less than the outstanding balances of the loans on them. Shoppers are often rolling these negative balances into new loans, which pushes up the new payment cost.

Automakers have also been more tightly aligning production with demand to avoid discounting vehicles. Incentives on average are about 6.6% of the average transaction price as of October, according to Cox Automotive. Leasing offers a way to get a car with a lower payment, but leasing rates are also down.

Vehicle trade-in data suggests that many buyers who are snapping up those fast-selling cheaper vehicles are either first-time buyers or are purchasing supplemental vehicles, Edmunds' Drury said.

The subcompact SUV segment is one lower-priced group that has seen sales rise in 2026. That is a segment that attracts a lot of first-time buyers. But this is still a relatively small share of the total car market, Fiorani said.

"It's not the mainstream vehicle that Americans want," he said. "It is the vehicle that entry-level buyers need to get to work, to get to school. ... It's people who don't have families yet."

Repeat buyers are less likely to want to step down into a compact car or SUV, Drury said.

"The moment you get into that other vehicle and it's a little bit smaller, it's not as comfortable, it doesn't fit all your stuff, all your kid's equipment for soccer practice, you're done," he said. "You're not giving up 360 cameras, ventilated seats, heated steering wheels, these niceties that you've become accustomed to. Automakers know that too. Dealers know that."

The truly cash-strapped are either snapping up the relatively few low-priced cars on dealer lots, moving to the used market, or holding on to their cars longer.

"The people who are trying to buy the smaller vehicles are really hurt by the way the economy is at the moment," Fiorani said. "So those people are trying to move into used vehicles or trying to maintain their current vehicle as long as they can. Automakers should be prepared for a softening in the marketplace."

#242. Swim and gym prices set to rise above inflation

Bosses at Perdiswell Leisure Centre in Worcester want to increase its fees from January

Inflation-busting price increases are on the way at a large Worcester leisure centre, with its operators blaming energy and staffing costs.

The price of an adult swim at Perdiswell Leisure Centre could rise 6.8% â from Â£7.30 to Â£7.80 â if the new charges are approved.

Fitness class fees would increase by 5.6% to Â£9.50, while the price of annual memberships would go up 4.9%.

Bosses at Freedom Leisure, the site's operator, said staffing and energy bills were rising faster than income as Worcester City Council were asked to approve the new prices.

A report on the price hikes, which outstrip the UK's 3.6% inflation rate, revealed energy costs had more than doubled in recent years, despite efforts to slash consumption.

If councillors sign them off in a meeting at the end of November, the average fee at Perdiswell Leisure Centre would increase by 5.3% from January.

Matt Wickham, from Freedom Leisure, said things are "tough" for the whole leisure industry

Hiring the artificial pitch at Perdiswell, which can currently be done for Â£44 an hour, would cost 10% more, although the council said it would still be significantly cheaper than comparable pitches elsewhere in the city.

Matt Wickham, Freedom Leisure's chief operating officer, explained the firm had seen "significant cost increases", with gas prices up by more than 50% over the second half of the year and minimum wages rising year-on-year.

"It's really tough because we know people are feeling the pinch in different ways, but our priority is all about offering a value-for-money service to people from all walks of life," he said.

He added the government should consider "immediate financial support" to help the leisure industry through the next 12 months and ongoing investment into leisure centre stock.

If approved, the increases would also apply at St John's Sports Centre and Nunnery Wood Sports Complex, both of which are owned by Freedom Leisure.

Gym-goers at Perdiswell had mixed views on the price increases â with some saying they were not surprised.

Sue Guest said: "I've had two hip replacements so I come and do the aqua swim, it's very beneficial to me.

"It would be a shame if they increase it, but then they (the operators) have their own expenses as well.

"I think it's still reasonable value for money, actually."

Colin English, a pensioner, said: "I use the gym quite a lot, I come at least four or five times a week.

"They put the price up last year â if they do it again by these sorts of amounts I may have to reconsider because it's getting expensive."

Another gym user called Vinny added: "It feels like everything is going up in price at the moment.

"I'd never stop going though, this gym is important to me so I really don't mind."

A final decision is expected to be made on 24 November, subject to discussions at two city council committees before then.

#238. Automakers like Rivian and Ford are ‘democratizing 3D printing’ to reduce costs and increase speed

DETROIT — Automakers including Ford Motor and Rivian Automotive are leaning into a new type of manufacturing line: 3D printing.

Manufacturers say it's helping them reduce costs and increase speed. The technology has been around for decades, but it's becoming more commoditized in the automotive industry, with 3D printers used to create everything from vehicle parts and accessories to tools for assembly plants and testing units for research and development labs.

"At this point, we don't have a choice but to scale up and begin using it through all factions of Rivian," Jonathan Dankenbring, Rivian senior manager of prototype manufacturing, told CNBC during a tour of the automaker's expansive 3D-printing operations in suburban Detroit. "We're going to be democratizing 3D printing for the entire company."

Also known as additive manufacturing, 3D printing can create three-dimensional objects from digital designs by dispensing material layer by layer, much like a traditional printer does with ink on paper. Instead of ink, the machines use a wide variety of materials such as plastics, composites, ceramics and even metals.

Printers can range from hundreds of dollars for hobby-like devices to hundreds of thousands of dollars for specialty equipment, depending on how big the product being produced is and the material it needs. They can produce individual pieces or hundreds of smaller parts in a batch using powders and resins.

Rivian said it has spent more than $5 million on 3D-printing machines, with the cost of materials for the operations growing to about $1.7 million annually.

Dankenbring said despite the costs, the machines typically pay for themselves within a year. He said the technology helps lower supplier tooling costs, streamline logistics expenses and cut production times for parts from weeks to days, if not hours.

"It's well in the millions [for savings], but the main thing is speed for Rivian," Dankenbring said.

The all-electric vehicle company's latest expansion of 3D printing includes adding new machines that can build flexible silicone parts as well as installing a bay of machines at its plant in Normal, Illinois, for employees to print parts on demand.

"3D prints should be readily available for every engineer so that they can make a part and get it in their hands as soon as possible," Dankenbring said.

Rivian has also used the technology for electric-motor models for fluid testing, creating screw guides for tooling and stamping press inserts and some production parts, including trim pieces to fill gaps in some early builds.

There's a similar push underway at larger, more-established automakers like Ford and its crosstown rival General Motors, which have used 3D printing for many years, including in their massive assembly plants.

Instead of having a bay of 3D-printing machines like at Rivian, Ford has departments for additive manufacturing at many of its plants to produce and distribute needed parts. That includes a new vending machine for 3D-printed parts for production of F-150 pickup trucks near its Dearborn, Michigan, headquarters.

"By putting the vending machine up, all the team leaders have access," said Gregory Foresi, a Ford mechanical engineer who ran Ford's 3D-printing lab at the plant from 2024 until last month. "We're able to keep that thing stocked rather than have a bunch of parts laying around in the lab. So that's been pretty huge."

Foresi used roughly two dozen machines to print off thousands of tools, some of which have been designed by employees, such as torque bit sockets, hood jackers and hand tools for smoothing adhesive.

Employees can use an app to request that specific auto parts or tools be printed. Ford's 3D-printing teams help develop them or determine what to produce next, as the machining time to build different parts can range from hours to days, if not weeks.

Foresi said creating the tools with 3D printing has allowed the automaker to uniquely design them when needed, get them faster and, in many cases, receive them at a lower cost.

Ford also has used 3D-printed parts to help keep production running. For example, Foresi said a large machining wheel was needed to maintain assembly line production and the company was able to 3D print the part until a permanent replacement from a supplier was available.

"They didn't perform as well, and it's not really a replacement for the ones that we were purchasing, but they kept the plant running," Foresi said.

Ford said it expects to have 3D labs deployed at more than 23 plants by the end of this year. Its current 3D-printing program has more than 200 machines, and there are additional printers outside the official program, the company said.

GM has used 3D-printed parts for some of its low-volume products in addition to research and development and inside plants, including recently expanding its use of industrial Stratasys printers that can cost hundreds of thousands of dollars based on use case, training and certification.

The Detroit automaker uses 135 components that are 3D-printed for its bespoke Cadillac Celestiq as well as other components for its performance Cadillac Blackwing sedans, including a unique medallion on the manual shifter knob.

Fadi Abro, senior global director of automotive and mobility at 3D-printing company Stratasys, said GM has been a great example of how companies can "standardize and then scale" 3D printing.

"The material properties improved so significantly that the use cases improved," he said, noting the company works with virtually every major automaker. "From a product perspective, we have to get the technology to become faster and more accessible, in an economic sense."

Correction: This story has been updated to reflect that Jonathan Dankenbring is Rivian's senior manager of prototype manufacturing. A previous version of this article misidentified him.

#237. What's happening to UK interest rates and what does it mean for mortgages?

Policymakers at the Bank of England are expected to raise UK interest rates for the first time in more than three years at their next meeting.

Most analysts believe the Bank rate will go up from 3.75% to 4%, following holds at the previous six meetings.

Before the US and Israel launched strikes against Iran in February, rates had been expected to fall this year, but the economic fallout from the conflict has pushed up inflation across the globe.

Interest rates affect mortgage, credit card and savings rates for millions of people.

An interest rate tells you how much it costs to borrow money, or the reward for saving it.

The Bank of England's base rate is what it charges other banks and building societies to borrow money, which influences what they charge their own customers for mortgages as well as the interest rate they pay on savings.

The Bank moves its base rate up and down in order to keep UK inflation â the rate at which prices are increasing â at or near 2%.

When inflation is above that target, the Bank typically puts rates up.

The idea is to encourage people to spend less, reducing demand for goods and services and limiting price rises.

The Bank of England's base rate rose to 5.25% in August 2023. It remained at that level until August 2024, when the Bank started cutting.

Five cuts brought rates down to 4%, before the Bank held rates at its meetings in September and November 2025.

It then cut in December 2025 before holding rates steady in January, March, April, June, July and September 2026.

Meanwhile, the main UK inflation measure, CPI, has fallen significantly since the October 2022 high of 11.1%, which was caused by Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine.

However, the rate has been heading back up and is expected to go higher. It was 3.5% in the year to September 2026, up from 3.1% the previous month and well above the central bank's 2% target.

The Office for National Statistics (ONS), which tracks UK inflation, blamed the acceleration on sharp rises in petrol and diesel prices.

The US-Israel war with Iran has put up energy and fuel costs around the world, which has increased the pace of price rises more generally.

At the start of the year, the Bank had been expected to cut interest rates twice in 2026, with the first drop predicted to come in March or April.

However, the increase in fuel prices and inflation after the outbreak of the conflict has upended all of this. Oil prices initially rose sharply as a result of disruption to supplies in the region, but dropped back when various ceasefires were agreed.

Oil prices rose again when the US and Iran resumed attacks in the Strait of Hormuz in July.

UK household energy bills rose after the increases in the price cap in July and October, while global oil prices have risen again recently.

Given the uncertainty, many analysts think UK interest rates will rise at the November meeting and again early next year.

What will the energy cap changes mean for my bills?

Just under a third of households have a mortgage, according to the government's English Housing Survey, external.

About 500,000 homeowners have a mortgage that "tracks" the Bank of England's rate.

Any rise in the Bank rate means an increase in the monthly repayments on their outstanding loan. For a typical loan, the cost would go up by about Â£31 a month if the Bank raises the base rate by 0.25 percentage points, but it depends on the size of people's loans.

An additional 500,000 homeowners on standard variable rates (SVRs) would wait to see if their lender chose to pass on any Bank rate rise. Typically, this would mean a Â£14 increase per month in repayments for a 0.25 percentage points rates rise.

But the vast majority of mortgage customers - some 87% - have fixed-rate deals. While their monthly payments aren't immediately affected by a rate change, their future deals are.

Most lenders have already priced in a rise in the Bank rate in November, so this is already reflected in any rates being offered.

As of 4 November, the average two-year fixed residential mortgage rate was at 5.96%, while the average five-year rate stood at 5.99%, according to financial information service Moneyfacts.

The average two-year tracker rate was 4.67%.

About 800,000 fixed-rate mortgages with an interest rate of 3% or below are expected to expire every year, on average, until the end of 2027. Borrowing costs for customers coming off those deals are likely to rise sharply.

You can see how your mortgage may be affected by future interest rate changes by using our calculator:

A modern browser with JavaScript and a stable internet connection is required to view this interactive.

At this rate, your payments could change byâ¦

The information you provided on your monthly payments would not be sufficient to pay off your mortgage within the number of years given.

It should be used as a guide only and does not represent the suitability, eligibility or availability of mortgage offers for users. For exact figures, users will need to approach an official mortgage lender.

Interest rates fluctuate based on the Bank of England's base rate and market conditions

If you can't see the mortgage calculator, click here

Bank of England interest rates also influence the amount charged on credit cards, new bank loans and car loans.

Lenders can decide to raise their own interest rates if Bank hikes make borrowing costs more expensive.

Equally, they can cut their own rates if Bank cuts make borrowing cheaper.

The Bank base rate also affects how much savers earn on their money.

A rising base rate is likely to mean an increase in the returns offered to savers by banks and building societies and vice versa.

As of 4 November, Moneyfacts said the average rate for an easy access savings account based on a balance of Â£10,000 was 2.58%. The average rate for an easy access cash ISA was 2.79%.

The average rate for customers who were prepared to lock their money away for a year was 4.48%.

Changes in rates particularly affect those who rely on the interest from their savings to top up their income.

In recent years, the UK has had one of the highest interest rates in the G7 - the group representing the world's seven largest so-called "advanced" economies.

In June 2024, the European Central Bank (ECB) started cutting the main interest rate for the eurozone from an all-time high of 4%, falling to 2% in June 2025.

However, in June 2026, the ECB raised rates to 2.25% as it reacted to the Iran war, before raising them again to 2.5% in September.

The US central bank - the Federal Reserve - raised rates for the first time in three years at its September meeting, the third under the new chair Kevin Warsh.

It meant rates rose to 3.75%-4% from 3.5%-3.75%, following three cuts since September 2025.

US President Donald Trump had repeatedly attacked the previous Fed chair Jerome Powell for not cutting rates.

Warsh said the rise was because "inflation is too high and has been for too long", adding that it was a "sober" and "responsible decision".

After the announcement, Trump expressed support for Warsh but said the Fed board, which votes on rate decisions, was "hostile".

#233. Why do veterinary fees and sausage rolls vary in price across London?

Cinnamon the rabbit needs to hop across London for cheaper vet fees

Have you ever wondered why the same item costs more or less depending on where in London you buy it?

Take a bog standard Greggsâ sausage roll for example. If you buy it in an outer London borough like Sutton, it costs Â£1.35. But if you buy it in central London you're likely to pay Â£1.60.

You might have guessed that the price difference, according to Greggs, is often about location.

"We use national pricing across most of our shops however, this can vary in certain locations where costs are higher than average, such as travel hubs," they told the BBC in a statement.

Sausage rolls from Greggs cost more in central London and at transport hubs than in outer London boroughs

You might think the price of a pizza at the same chain would be the same wherever in London you eat it.

A Duo Romana pizza from Pizza Express costs Â£20.95 in Muswell Hill, north London.

But three miles (5km) further north in Southgate, the same pizza at Pizza Express costs a pound more.

And in central London the same pizza will set you back Â£22.95.

Pizza Express was asked for comment but did not respond.

Consumer expert Helen Dewdney from the Complaining Cow, external website says that as well as higher costs in central London, the price difference can also be due to how convenient it is to get to the restaurant.

Southgate has a Tube station, Muswell Hill does not.

"Itâs all about cashing in on convenience for people.

"One will be nearer a Tube, so theyâre counting on people saying 'Oh, I'll just get a pizza on the way home'.

"Whereas another you might have to make a journey to it."

Dewdney from The Complaining Cow advises shopping around and using price comparison apps to get the best prices

Other examples of cross-borough price variations include:

A Vue cinema ticket bought online costs Â£6.99 in Eltham, but Â£9.99 in Piccadilly or Angel, Islington

A blow dry at Rush hair salon costs from Â£45 in Kentish Town, north London but Â£40 in Ilford, east London

Fish, chips and a drink in Wetherspoons in Sidcup, south-east London costs Â£11.18 but the same meal in Camden Town, north London costs Â£12.77.

Also, petrol prices can vary from pump to pump at the same company and within as little as a mile (1.5km) radius.

A recent example from EG On the Move on the same day in London found these price variations:

Unleaded (E10): 171.9p â 177.9p per litre

Other forecourts also charge more or less if they are near a main road or a motorway or part of a supermarket for example.

For this Dewdney recommends downloading a price comparison app like petrolprices.com, external

"You can look at the distance as well as the price difference so youâre not driving too far away," she explained.

"Itâs not always the nearest one thatâs worth going to."

The price list for veterinary treatments at Pets At Home in Chingford where a rabbit castration costs Â£136

Recent changes to legislation mean that vets must publish a comprehensive price list for standard services but it does not necessarily follow that vets in a different practice at the same chain will have the same prices.

Vets for Pets in Pets At Home is a case in point.

In Pets At Home in Chingford, east London the Vets for Pets practice charges Â£136 for a rabbit castration including two follow-up consultations.

The same treatment at Vets For Pets in Pets At Home in Enfield, north London costs Â£70.

Dewdney admits being surprised that the price can almost double from one practice to another.

"I think it's an interesting one, because they're saying it's a franchise and they can set their own prices.

"Although the government did bring in some changes for vets, it wasn't about capping the prices. It was just that they had to be more transparent," she explained.

Cinnamon the rabbit is just glad he cannot be castrated twice

Pets At Home said: "Vets for Pets practices are independently owned and operated, with practice owners having full clinical and day-to-day operational autonomy, including setting fees for delivering the high-quality care and services they provide.

"Pricing decisions are made locally and vary for many reasons including factors such as operating costs, staffing, facilities and the unique needs of the communities they serve.

"All costs are communicated clearly with clients ahead of any treatment or service being provided."

The company also said that veterinary practices had, like many businesses across the UK "experienced increased operating costs over the past year, including rises in energy, wages, medicines and other essential supplies".

It added: "These pressures are felt right across the sector, but practice teams work hard to keep costs as low as possible to ensure high-quality care is accessible and affordable for pet owners in their local communities."

Perhaps to shop around, and not to assume that prices at the same chain will be the same wherever you go, whether that be for veterinary care, your weekly shop, having your hair done or filling up the car.

Price comparison websites can be a useful tool, and it is worth remembering that just because goods may be from the same restaurant, supermarket or petrol station in a different area, prices may well vary.

Or as Dewdney summed up: "People have just got to do their research."

#232. Baked by Melissa enters Target with first at-home cupcake mix

Baked by Melissa will enter the at-home baking aisle in Target stores as the retailer tries to persuade shoppers to spend more at its locations this holiday season.

As of Sunday, Target is exclusively selling the brand's cupcake kits, liners and a cupcake pan, all in the dessert company's signature bite-size portions. The line includes three cupcake flavors: Christmas Tie-Dye, Marshmallow Hot Cocoa and Red Velvet.

The company's entry into at-home baking comes as a number of restaurants turn to consumer packaged goods to boost sales in an otherwise challenging time for the industry.

Some players, such as California Pizza Kitchen and TGI Fridays, have licensed their brands for frozen foods for decades. But other restaurant chains, including Chick-fil-A and Dutch Bros, have leaned into grocery products much more recently. For smaller companies, such as Momofuku or Carbone, space on a grocery shelf helps extend brand reach and gives consumers a chance to try their food without a reservation and at a much lower cost.

Founded by Melissa Ben-Ishay in 2008, Baked by Melissa has nine permanent locations in the New York area and ships its minicupcakes nationwide.

The regional chain has garnered attention outside the city thanks to Ben-Ishay's social media presence. On TikTok, the Baked by Melissa account has more than 2.8 million followers, fueled by popular recipe videos, such as for her green goddess salad dressing. Ben-Ishay's social media stardom has led to collaborations with Ferrero and Wonder.

Baked by Melissa had discussed at-home baking products for years, but Ben-Ishay, who is also president of the company, only decided to take the plunge a year ago during a trip to Target with her two kids, she told CNBC. A spin through the baking aisle made her decide Baked by Melissa "should be here," she said.

"I love baking cake mix. I truly do. And, quite frankly, sometimes I just think it tastes better," Ben-Ishay said.

But retail moves more slowly than Baked by Melissa's usual pace.

The dessert company needed just a few days to create New York Knicks-branded minicupcakes after the team won the NBA championship in June, for example, but figuring out retail products meant "a lot of waiting and approvals and new partners," Ben-Ishay said.

Twelve months later, the cupcake mixes have arrived on store shelves, just in time for the all-important holiday season.

For Target, the next two months will be critical as the retailer tries to woo back its core customer after a disappointing holiday season last year. That prototypical Target shopper often looks a lot like Ben-Ishay.

"We started with Target because I'm a 42-year-old mom of two, and I find myself there a lot," she said.

#230. From tanks to drones: South Korea’s defense firms face a new battlefield test

South Korean defense stocks rode a wave of global rearmament over the past two years.

Russia’s invasion of Ukraine accelerated military spending across Europe. South Korean companies drew buyers with weapons that were competitively priced and could be delivered relatively quickly, including tanks, artillery and missile systems.

But that rally has given way to a sharp sell-off, with shares of several major South Korean defense companies plunging this year.

The combined market value of the top four Korean defense companies — Hanwha Aerospace, LIG D&A, Hyundai Rotem, and Korea Aerospace — had plunged to about 62 trillion won ($46.18 billion) as of end-October from their 52-week peak of 170 trillion won, according to South Korean broadcaster SBS.

Are South Korean companies, known for exporting tanks and artillery systems, well-placed for a modern battlefield that has seen drones and counter-drone systems proliferate in Ukraine and Iran?

The war in Ukraine has shown how relatively inexpensive drones can threaten expensive military equipment, and drone strikes from Iran have depleted stocks of costly air defense missiles of the U.S. and its Gulf allies.

"Modern warfare is being fought with less traditional weaponry (tanks, cannons, armored vehicles, etc.), which Koreans are positioned for. Even on this front, drones, for example, are dominated by China, pricing out other potential competitors from Europe, the U.S., and Asia," said Peter Kim, senior managing director at KB Securities.

KJ Hwang, Korea autos & industrials analyst, at Bank of America agreed that procurement priorities were evolving, with more focus on drones, counter-drone systems and air defense.

The reversal in defense stocks is not all down to new warfighting platforms.

The declines come as investors weigh several concerns: disappointing earnings, a stronger won and delayed export orders.

Fund flows into the semiconductor sector and high valuations built on expectations of strong earnings growth have also weighed on Korean defense shares, said KB Securities' Kim, adding that governments have moved slower than expected to convert defense spending pledges into approved budgets and orders.

Financial results have amplified those concerns. Hanwha Aerospace’s third-quarter operating profit fell over 46% from a year earlier, with the company citing delivery-schedule adjustments and one-off costs. Its shares are down more than 28% so far this year.

Hyundai Rotem, which reported a more than 11.1% decline in operating profit, has seen its stock drop almost 60%.

Korea Aerospace Industries’ third-quarter sales and operating profit improved about 52% and 32% respectively, limiting the decline in its shares to just over 8% this year.

Experts, however, are still positive on the sector, especially over the long-term.

"South Korean defense companies have continued to expand cooperation with partners in Western Europe and the Middle East, including in air-defense systems, while additional K9 howitzer orders suggest that supply constraints and the continued importance of deterrence remain supportive of demand for conventional platforms," BofA's Hwang said.

Some deals made this year include K9 orders to Spain from Hanwha Aerospace, valued at about 546 million euros, as well as a $922 million order for K239 rocket systems from Norway.

LIG D&A has made a $94 million deal with Malaysia for its K-SAAM naval surface-to-air guided missile system, the first export deal of the system ever made.

Countries still rely heavily on conventional weapons to bolster their defense capabilities, with KB Securities' Kim saying that the Korean defense sector "remains attractive, given the long-term growth outlook is still in the teens."

David Roche, strategist at Quantum Strategy, told CNBC that there is a "greater emphasis" on AI and drone technology in military orders as major powers learn the lessons of Ukraine, however, “drones cannot capture and hold terrain ... So, in any mobile battlefield, infantry, tanks, armor etc, are essential."

South Korean defense companies are also actively investing in drones and unmanned systems, and while meaningful exports will take time as overseas buyers require a proven operational track record, Kim expects more tangible results potentially emerging in the near future.

#228. The AI telling farmers when to harvest

Choosing the right moment to harvest can be a make-or-break decision

When the first day of the apple harvest rolled around in Washington State last year, the fruit was ripe and the pickers were ready â but the weather had other ideas.

"It was like 38Câ¦ it's not safe for people to work in that heat," recalls Joel Carter at Okanagan Specialty Fruits. "We had to stop at 10 o'clock in the morning."

He says that artificial intelligence (AI) models that forecast ideal harvest dates, taking into account the weather, would be useful. "You need to know more than just when your fruit is going to be ripe. How long do you have to pick it?" he says. "That's where these models are really helpful."

Carter's company has more than 1,250 acres of apple orchards in Washington, and the fruit is grown for sliced apple portions â often sold to hotels and schools, for example. The firm is investing in technology with the hope of maximising productivity. Even the apples are genetically engineered so that they don't brown easily once cut.

But planning a harvest is tricky. New tools that count and analyse fruit on the tree or vine, and predict when crops will ripen, are emerging. It matters because prices for fruit, especially high-value berries such as strawberries or blueberries can fluctuate wildly. Getting the harvest date wrong means you book seasonal workers when you don't actually need them, and risk missing out on the biggest profits.

Okanagan Specialty Fruits is already experimenting with cameras from a Canadian firm called Vivid Machines. The cameras are mounted atop tractors and they scoop up imagery of the apple trees as the tractor trundles by. AI identifies buds, flowers or fruit in that footage.

"Right now, Vivid is telling us crop estimates and harvest dates," says Carter. He notes that the system is good at picking out very tiny flower buds, which are hard to see at a glance with the naked eye.

But the accuracy of forecasts is noticeably dependent on the quality of historical information fed in to the system, adds Carter. "This isn't something where an AI can scrape the internet and figure out what's the average [yield] for Granny Smith," he explains. "It's going to be bespoke to your farm."

Apples are at least somewhat forgiving â the harvest window for those Granny Smiths is three weeks long, says Carter. For other fruit, such as berries, you might only have a few days.

"If a strawberry crop is on, you have to harvest it â otherwise your entire crop gets diseased very, very quickly," says Raymond Martin, co-founder and chief operating officer of FruitCast, a UK company that offers harvest forecasts to fruit growers here.

His firm offers growers crop predictions for strawberries and also raspberries, blackberries, blueberries and tomatoes. "We're moving on to grapes next year," adds Martin.

Don't experienced farmers know when their fruit will be ripe, I ask? Martin says they generally do â but not necessarily across their entire farm, which might be many acres in size, or have both outdoor and indoor growing areas. "We do exactly what the farmers could do but we just do it on a scale that they can't."

The system analyses footage of ripening fruit that can be captured by drones, someone walking up and down a field with a smartphone, or a camera mounted on a farm vehicle.

This year has been challenging, notes Martin, with hot weather and severe drought hitting much of the UK. This has stressed many fruit plants, putting them into thermal dormancy and slowing down fruit production.

There's only a small window for picking berries says Raymond Martin

The FruitCast model accounts for weather and irrigation conditions in its forecasts. The company says, "our forecasts land within 10% of actual picked volume one week out (90% accurate) and within 17% three weeks out (83% accurate). We guarantee less than 20% errorâ¦"

Angus Soft Fruits is one company that has worked with FruitCast. AI tech for forecasting fruit ripeness is not yet "a finished solution" says operations director Neill Finlayson: "This journey is still ongoing and, whilst significant progress has been made, the industry remains some way from achieving a fully integrated forecasting ecosystem."

Driscoll's, a California headquartered fruit seller with a big operation in the UK, also confirmed to BBC News that some of its independent fruit growers in the UK have used FruitCast's technology.

Yasaman Ghasempour (left) is developing ripeness detection techniques

Researchers are also exploring new ways of analysing fruit in intense detail. Many farmers already use handheld brix meters to gauge the sugar content of their crop. These devices work by measuring how much light has changed direction, or refracted, after passing through a liquid â indicating the volume of solids present.

Some such meters use infrared light, meaning there's no need to cut into the fruit to take measurements. But Yasaman Ghasempour at Princeton University says millimetre waves, high frequency radio waves, can penetrate deeper "They basically respond very well to humidity, water [and sugar]," she says.

She and her students have come up with a millimetre wave-based ripeness detector, external that could be used by farmers â or even customers searching for perfectly ripe fruit in shops. Her students tried it out at a local market in New Jersey. "[Staff] there got kind of scared that we were doing something shady," laughs Ghasempour.

Such fine-grained analysis of fruit, if currently unripe, could also help inform forecasts of when it will be ripe.

Jing Zhang says new tech has to prove its worth to farmers

But while some growers, such as Carter, are interested in trying out new tech, many will wonder whether investing in it is really worthwhile. "Adoption is very complicated," says Jing Zhang at North Carolina State University. "The grower has to have confidence in the research and whether or not it works."

Zhang has been working on a system to automatically count the number of blueberries, external captured in smartphone images of blueberry bushes.

The simplest systems need not be expensive. Kevin Wang at the University of Florida has developed another crop-counting tool that can harvest imagery, external from $100 (Â£74) drones.

In the future, such information could feed in to ripeness predictions. However, he points out that some farmers might be uneasy about sharing commercially sensitive information about their growing strategies, such as fertiliser and irrigation plans, with third parties and AI models.

Ben Palone is senior director of automation and commercialisation at Western Growers, an association that represents farmers in the western US. He says harvest forecasts have some potential as "an optimisation tool".

But farmers will likely continue to rely on human intelligence. "Growers like to have people in the mix to make some of those very critical decisions â especially when it comes to harvests," he says.

The critical tech staying safe by going underground

Why airlines are warning over lithium-ion batteries
