#119. Pound rises to its highest level since the Brexit referendum

The pound has risen to its highest overall level since the Brexit referendum over a decade ago.

Sterling has risen notably in recent weeks against the euro and the dollar, ahead of an expected rise in interest rates.

The most-watched measure of the value of the British currency against a basket of 18 of the world's main currencies this week reached its joint highest level since the June 2016 referendum, according to Bank of England figures.

The value reached 86.4 on an index where 100 equals the value in 2005. The Bank will officially confirm the calculation on Thursday.

The value of sterling fell significantly after the Brexit referendum in June 2016, and reached further lows during the Brexit process, the Covid pandemic and the 2022 mini-Budget.

Some traders said the UK was being seen in a different light given relative political stability, solid growth and the rapprochement with the European Union.

Sterling has responded strongly to Brexit developments and political uncertainty in recent years.

Jim O'Neill, an economist and former Treasury minister, said: "I'm not surprised. People were, and to a lesser extent, are, underestimating the improving momentum in the UK."

A stronger pound can help rein in inflation, and increase buying power for consumers on imports and on holidays.

UK growth so far this year has outperformed all forecasts, but there are signs that higher energy and fuel prices have now hit consumer confidence.

The Bank's nine-member Monetary Policy Committee faces an important decision on interest rates on Thursday, with the first rise in three years widely expected.

The pound was up 0.13% against the dollar at $1.3495 and 0.16% lower against the euro at â¬1.1979 as of 04:15 GMT on Thursday.

Interest rates expected to rise for first time in over three years

#116. Interest rates hiked to 4% in first increase in over three years

Interest rates have risen for the first time in more than three years and the Bank of England has signalled a further increase is likely unless the US-Iran war is resolved.

Rates rose to 4% from 3.75% in a bid to curb inflation mainly driven by the conflict's impact on oil prices.

Andrew Bailey, the Bank's governor, said the decision "shouldn't be seen as the first in an inevitable long sequence of hikes", but warned "unless the conflict in the Middle East is resolved, neither does it seem likely to be the last increase".

The higher level will increase the cost of borrowing for things such as mortgages, loans and credit cards, but will also lead to higher returns on savings.

The Bank's rate-setting committee all backed increasing interest rates, with eight out of nine voting in favour of the rise to 4%. One member wanted an even bigger increase to 4.25%.

Bailey said the rate hike was warranted due to higher energy prices, but stressed it was not the start of a series of rate rises.

The governor pointed to what happens in the Middle East as the main factor in future decisions.

"Put simply, it requires real progress towards settling the conflict in the Middle East," he said.

Bailey said he could "see the argument" for a further rate hike, before taking a "more conditional approach, dependent on the evidence on energy prices and inflation dynamics".

However, members of the rate-setting committee offered different views on the path forward.

Clare Lombardelli agreed there was "likely to be a case for a further increase in rates in the near term", but Megan Greene said any decision would depend on how inflation impacts wages, prices and the jobs market.

Alan Taylor said the rate rise was a "a risk-management 'one-for-now' insurance hike".

Inflation is well below its peak of a few years ago but has been heading back up and is expected to go higher.

It was 3.5% in the year to September 2026, up from 3.1% the previous month and well above the central bank's 2% target.

The ongoing impact of the Iran war, primarily on the cost of energy and fuel, has filtered through to the UK inflation rate. Millions of domestic energy customers are braced for a hefty rise in energy bills in January.

The Bank now expects inflation to peak at 4.9% in the middle of next year, higher than it originally estimated, and not return to its target of 2% until 2028.

In what it called an "adverse scenario", where the US-Iran war could escalate further and send oil and gas prices much higher, inflation could reach 6.2% in 2027, it suggested.

While the UK cannot control global energy prices, the Bank is raising rates in order to limit the knock-on effects of high energy prices resulting in inflation going up across the board.

The Bank of England last raised rates in August 2023.

Its base rate is what it charges other banks and building societies to borrow money, which influences what they charge their own customers for mortgages as well as the interest rate they pay on savings.

Higher fuel prices can lead to businesses passing on higher costs to customers.

So far there was "limited evidence" this had happened for goods, such as food, with some firms absorbing cost increases and accepting lower margins, the Bank found.

The hike on Thursday will see 510,000 tracker mortgage-holders see a Â£31 monthly jump in repayments, according to banking trade body UK Finance.

Homeowners on fixed deals will see no change, though future products may be affected. How people on variable deals will be impacted depending on how their lender reacts. Typically a 0.25pp rise means a Â£14 increase per month in repayments.

#113. World's largest olive oil company plummets 20% after rivals agree to takeover deal

Shares of Deoleo, the world's largest olive oil company, plummeted Thursday after two rivals announced plans to acquire the company, a deal that would further consolidate Spain's dominance in the sector.

Spanish olive oil firms Dcoop and Acesur will each hold a 50% stake in the company, resulting in a new olive oil giant in the region.

The deal is intended to boost olive oil consumption worldwide and support farmers in creating value through the supply chain, Dcoop said in an announcement.

Deoleo was last trading down 19.9%. However the stock is up around 55% since the beginning of the year as it's benefited from a moderation in olive oil prices that has helped lift U.S. sales.

CNBC reached out to Deoleo for comment and is awaiting a response.

The news comes after an intensifying takeover battle between Dcoop and multinational firms including those from Italy, France, and Australia. The stock gained over 20% on Aug. 19 when news of the takeover battle emerged. Dcoop reportedly previously offered 470 million euros ($545 million) for Deoleo.

Spain is one of the world's leading olive oil producers and is often used as a global reference for prices.

Prices for the commodity have been volatile over the past few years as supplies swung dramatically one season to the next, with climate change, water scarcity, and pest and disease playing a role.

Deoleo CEO Cristóbal Valdés told CNBC in July that the period between 2022 and 2024 was one of the most challenging periods for the sector, but that prices are now stabilizing due to favourable rain trends.

"The highly complex market cycle experienced between 2022 and 2024, which had a severe yet temporary impact on the industry, is now definitively behind us," Valdés said at the time.

#110. Graduates are managing AI - but are they learning much else?

Managing AI agents is a big part of Lotte Underwood's job

Recent university graduate Lotte Underwood is a public relations executive with a twist - she spends more time using AI than actually doing PR.

Underwood, 25, joined London-based PR agency Fight or Flight in November last year, in the new role of "AI associate" after completing a degree and then a masters in marketing.

She's part of a recently established, dedicated AI team that has built around 30 AI agents. Specific tasks include industry research, client reporting, media interview preparation, helping clients create clearer briefs, and image generation.

A big part of Underwood's job is to manage those agents, check their outputs and feed those to the team members who are dealing directly with clients and the media.

Another key responsibility of hers is to monitor how clients appear in the responses from AI tools like ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and others.

"A huge part is being in client meetings and calls to work out what is actually going to be most helpful from these agents," says Underwood.

AI is seen as one of the main factors behind graduate job cuts, but companies are also creating dedicated AI-specific graduate roles like Underwood's.

Dillan Patel, for example, joined engineering software company Stride 18 months ago as an intern. A business management graduate, he was put to work developing an internal chatbot designed to capture the knowledge of a senior staff member transitioning into retirement.

Patel, 24, is now an AI automation analyst, tasked with developing further internal agents to provide specific information to individual teams, while also identifying which manual or repetitive tasks across the company could be done by AI.

"My role focuses on finding practical ways to use technology, automation, data and AI to improve how we operate," says Coventry-based Patel.

Debra Maxwell is looking for "AI disruptors"

Such AI savvy graduates are also in demand from companies like ArvatoConnect, an outsourced contact centre company.

CEO Debra Maxwell says the company's usual graduate intake is on hold this year, but she plans to resume it next year with a bigger focus on AI.

"We would be looking for more AI-disruptor type roles, where you can challenge us as an organisation to think more holistically about where AI can be applied and where we could grow our business from," says London-based Maxwell.

"We see our graduates as not filling administrative roles, but actually adding value to the organisation in a different way."

Maxwell adds: "For example, one of our recent graduates is using AI to do deep dives on specific markets, and provide more information for when we're bidding [for new contracts]. It's basically giving him a more rounded role."

If AI can make a graduate job bigger and more integral to the organisation, Maxwell argues that companies can create a more loyal, engaged workforce that is able to contribute more meaningfully, much earlier.

Research from consultancy PwC found that some graduate roles are assuming more responsibilities because of AI, as it allows companies to demand more senior abilities from junior hires.

For the consultancy's 2026 AI Jobs Barometer, external, it analysed 2.4 million entry-level jobs in the US. It found that the entry-level roles most influenced by AI are now seven times more likely to require traditionally senior-level skills. These include motivational leadership, strategic decision making and team building.

Those entry-level roles with higher expectations have in fact grown 35% since 2019, PwC found, while other entry-level roles actually shrank by 10%.

Cian Clinton uses around ten AI agents for work tasks

Cian Clinton, who joined consultancy Valliance last September after graduating from a Bachelor of Science in 2024, says the incorporation of AI into his role makes him feel less like a graduate, and more like an equal team member. He uses around 10 AI agents daily for different tasks.

"Your ideas and your creativity are a lot more valued because you actually get to implement those firsthand, and the responsibility you take on is a lot greater because of that," says London-based Clinton, 23.

"I am now taking on responsibilities which would usually take me four or five years to get to."

His career progression, he believes, will now be faster as a result.

"AI is killing the 'pyramid' approachâ¦progression is no longer based on your knowledge bank. Now it's more based on your critical thinking and understanding of the processes," says Clinton.

There's no doubt Underwood, Patel and Clinton are developing valuable AI knowledge which can help them across their careers.

But with their time at work so focused on AI, does it matter how much - or how little - time they're spending learning about the professional field they're supposed to be operating in?

Underwood says she'd like to still pick up the traditional PR skills her role isn't currently focused on, but is confident of her future in the agency's growing AI team.

"There's certain things that I haven't had the chance to do yet, like directly pitching to the media. I absolutely plan to do this," she says.

Graduates becoming too AI-centric is an issue for Simon Pratt, chief operating officer at Portman Finance Group. He says being able to use AI effectively is important, but the company's two-year graduate programme is fully centred on "learning the trade".

"Our graduates rotate across four areas at six-month intervals, working directly alongside senior relationship managers to learn their trade," Northampton-based Pratt explains.

"They'll learn all of our financial products, how to draft credit papers for submission to lenders, and how to build meaningful relationships with our customers. They'll progress to running transactions from start to finish."

Claire Reid, PwC UK's chief technology and innovation officer, believes graduate roles that are too AI heavy won't necessarily provide graduates long-term career progression.

"There will be no graduate job now that doesn't require a baseline of AI literacy," says Glasgow-based Reid.

"But AI skills on their own are not going to help you in your career. You've got to couple that with that sector knowledge of what that client actually wants."

Back in Coventry, Dillan Patel is confident his AI work will continue to be valuable, and he wants to keep building on it.

"I don't think I'd want to explore different positions within the business. Through my involvement in AI-related projects, I already work closely with different areas of the business and I'm gaining valuable knowledge and experience along the way," he says.

#109. Banks are turning cautious on lending, according to the Fed. What it means for the economy

U.S. banks are starting to tighten their lending standards amid growing concern over the underlying health of the economy, according to a new Federal Reserve survey.

The Fed's latest Senior Loan Officer Opinion Survey on Bank Lending Practices for October, published Nov. 2, showed an uneven credit backdrop in the third quarter, with smaller businesses, riskier households and commercial property in particular facing a lending squeeze.

The latest quarterly SLOOS study for October — which pulled together responses from 61 domestic banks and 18 U.S. branches of foreign lenders — covered changes in lending standards, terms and demand during the third quarter.

The survey tracks banks' reported lending standards, terms and demand, not actual loan volumes, offering a snapshot of evolving credit conditions and sentiment.

Commercial and industrial lending to large and middle-market firms was broadly unchanged from the previous survey — but banks tightened lending to smaller businesses.

"Banks reported tightening standards for premiums charged on riskier loans for firms of all sizes," the report noted. Lenders pointed to a less favorable or more uncertain economic outlook, and a worsening of industry-specific problems as the key reason for the shift.

Commercial real estate is also emerging as a pressure point, with banks reporting tightened standards across the board on construction and land development, nonfarm nonresidential properties, and multifamily properties, and weakening demand for loans in all CRE categories.

U.S. banks are also tightening credit standards for nonbank financial firms such as private equity funds and business and mortgage credit intermediaries. The pullback comes amid growing demand for liquidity from private equity funds and business credit intermediaries, with lenders again highlighting prevailing economic uncertainty and concerns over the deteriorating financial strength among NDFI borrowers.

Meanwhile, household credit sentiment appears mixed.

While banks have left mortgage standards broadly unchanged, there has been some easing for jumbo mortgages — but tighter rules for subprime loans — as banks reported weaker demand, on balance, across all residential real estate loan categories.

"Banks reported being more likely to approve credit card applications to super-prime and prime borrowers and less likely to approve applications to subprime borrowers, with small banks driving the trend for subprime borrowers," the report added.

The squeeze comes as borrowing costs moved sharply higher during the third quarter. The 10-year Treasury note yield — the main benchmark for mortgages, auto loans and credit card debt — hit an intraday high of 5.349% on Oct. 5, its highest level since April 2002, amid concerns over rising U.S. debt.

Tiffany Wilding, managing director and economist at PIMCO, said small businesses, housing and debt-laden firms face tighter credit and higher borrowing costs as the AI investment boom squeezes other parts of the U.S. economy.

"Higher government bond yields have catalyzed a broader repricing of credit risk, especially for lower-quality borrowers," Wilding said in a note Oct. 28.

Signals of credit stress among riskier households chime with a recent CNBC and SurveyMonkey poll, which found that one in five Americans who rent or have a mortgage spend at least half of their pretax income on housing, while 49% spend between 25% and 49%. Lender guidance recommends consumers spend 28% to 30% of their income on housing.

A separate Federal Reserve report indicated that U.S. families' ability to address their debts has worsened over the past three years. The Fed's latest Survey of Consumer Finances, released Oct. 9, found that the percentage of families behind on loan payments at the end of 2025 reached almost 20%, up from about 12% in the previous survey in 2022.

#107. CVS Health plans to split itself up. Here's why a breakup could make sense now

CVS Health plans to break itself up — and this time, the bet looks a lot more viable.

The healthcare conglomerate on Wednesday announced plans to split into two publicly traded companies by the first half of 2028. CVS would separate its pharmacy business – including roughly 9,000 retail locations – from its major insurance business and pharmacy benefit manager, Caremark.

The move comes more than two years after CVS considered a breakup but ultimately walked away from the idea. At the time, analysts questioned whether the retail pharmacy business could survive on its own.

“Back then, the idea of retail standing alone was unthinkable,” Brian Tanquilut, senior equity research analyst at Jefferies, told CNBC.

But the economics of the businesses have changed: CVS's retail pharmacy business has become more consistently profitable, while its insurer, Aetna, has improved its performance despite persistently high medical costs in Medicare Advantage plans. That could make it easier for the company to separate the units — and for investors to recognize the value of each on its own, rather than valuing them together as one sprawling healthcare conglomerate.

“For us, it's showing the stability of the pharmacy,” Michael Cherny, senior research analyst at Leerink Partners, said in an interview. “This business is a much different business from the one that's been considered three years ago, five years ago, 10 years ago, where there had been a litany of headwinds that the pharmacy just couldn't overcome.”

CVS CEO David Joyner told CNBC’s "Squawk on the Street" on Wednesday that back in 2024, the company was "in a turnaround" without the flexibility, cash or business performance to execute a split. Now, CVS has posted eight consecutive quarters of beating Wall Street earnings estimates and has raised its guidance several times over the last two years, thanks to improvement across its PBM, pharmacy and insurer.

Since taking the helm in 2024, Joyner has pursued an aggressive turnaround plan that involves $2 billion in cost cuts, store closures, leadership shakeups and efforts to rein in Medicare Advantage costs.

The company also now expects to generate at least $15.5 billion in operating cash flow in 2026, a roughly 70% increase from $9.1 billion in 2024, and says it has reached its leverage target ahead of schedule.

"What's changed in two years is the fact that we now can look at this business and the assets that are assembled and really determine, without any distress or without anything that we have to do, what is the right strategic decision to make," Joyner told analysts on a call Wednesday, adding that the decision comes "out of strength."

While investors have largely cheered CVS's progress in recent years, Wall Street appeared more pessimistic about Wednesday's announcement, as the company's shares dropped about 12%.

CVS's retail business has benefited from changes in the competitive landscape, including the widespread retrenchment and store closures of Walgreens, Rite Aid and other pharmacy operators over the last few years. That’s created opportunities for CVS to gain market share, some analysts told CNBC.

CVS has also introduced changes to its pharmacy reimbursement model. Its CostVantage program is designed to replace the traditional process with a more transparent, cost-based model. Some analysts say that has helped the company better align reimbursement with the cost of dispensing prescriptions and contributed to steadier profits.

Those developments represent a meaningful shift for a business that has faced years of pressure from declining prescription reimbursement, store closures and competition from online pharmacies and other retailers.

That doesn't mean the retail pharmacy has become a high-growth business. CVS's retail pharmacy segment remains a relatively low-margin operation, with adjusted operating margins of roughly 4% to 5%. But analysts say it may now be stable and profitable enough to operate independently – even if investors value it at a discount to CVS's other businesses.

In a note Wednesday, RBC Capital Markets analyst Ben Hendrix said the lower valuation investors assign to the retail pharmacy unit has weighed on CVS's overall value and obscured the earnings strength of businesses like Aetna and Caremark.

The underlying growth of the insurance and PBM businesses is also stronger than that of retail pharmacy, Tanquilut said, after accounting for changes to Caremark's rebate arrangements.

Aetna in particular has gotten a better handle on medical costs by exiting unprofitable markets, expanding value-based care models, and tightening utilization management.

The breakup also comes as Caremark faces mounting scrutiny over PBM practices, including state-level legal and regulatory challenges involving the businesses and their relationships with pharmacies.

Republicans in Tennessee and Arkansas have passed laws that would ban any one company from owning both a pharmacy and a PBM — thought the Arkansas measure has been held up in court. Cherny noted that legal hurdles involving PBMs in other states have continued to pile up.

“Through what seems to be a never-ending litany of state lawsuits, it feels like it's been under more pressure,” Cherny said. “It's unclear if it just became, for lack of a better term, too tenuous to move forward with those two entities combined as one. I’m not sure.”

Even so, Joyner told the Wall Street Journal that the company wasn’t splitting up because of regulatory issues. CVS has said the separation follows a strategic review and is intended to give each business greater focus and flexibility.

The move also marks a significant departure from the integrated healthcare strategy CVS spent years building.

CVS spent roughly $70 billion to acquire Aetna in 2018 and argued that combining health insurance, pharmacy benefit management, retail pharmacies and health providers under one roof would help coordinate care and create a competitive advantage. CVS was still promoting that model as recently as its investor day last year.

The proposed split suggests the company now sees more value in separating at least some of those businesses – though it is not abandoning vertical integration altogether.

Aetna and Caremark will remain together in the healthcare company, alongside CVS’s specialty pharmacy, its primary care clinic Oak Street Health and its home-visit provider Signify Health. The separate pharmacy company will include roughly 9,000 retail stores, mail pharmacy operations and MinuteClinic, a retail medical clinic located inside certain CVS locations.

Both sides are large enough that they can stand on their own, Cherny said. Caremark is one of the country's three largest PBMs, while CVS has one of the largest retail pharmacy footprints in the U.S.

But separating the company raises questions about how much of the commercial relationship between the businesses can survive.

The relationship has historically benefited both sides. As it stands, Caremark can use CVS's extensive retail footprint to offer members convenient access to prescriptions and steer them toward CVS locations through preferred pharmacy arrangements. Programs such as Maintenance Choice, for example, give eligible members the option to fill certain maintenance medications at CVS retail pharmacies or through mail order.

Those arrangements also helped direct prescription volume to CVS's stores, giving the retail pharmacy unit an important source of customers. Because the businesses shared a parent company, the financial benefits of that relationship ultimately went to CVS as a whole.

In a note, RBC's Hendrix also raised questions about how the split could impact Aetna's Medicare Advantage quality ratings, which determine whether health plans qualify for bonus payments from the government. Retail pharmacists have played a role in engaging Aetna members, and a separation could complicate that relationship.

But Joyner told analysts Wednesday that the synergy between the two businesses would be "preserved in commercial agreements between the two organizations," adding that "we don't need the integration of an operating structure."

Joyner said CVS will be working on those potential agreements over the next 15 to 18 months.

Preferred pharmacy networks are common across the industry, and separate ownership does not prevent Caremark from continuing to favor CVS pharmacies through negotiated arrangements, Cherny said. The difference is that under separate ownership, the profits from those arrangements would no longer accrue to a single company, he noted.

“The issue is not whether the companies can continue working together in some capacity, but whether any future contractual arrangements preserve the SAME competitive advantages and flexibility as today under common ownership,” Cherny said in an afternoon note on Wednesday.

Still, both businesses face challenges on their own.

Tanquilut cautioned that some of CVS's recent retail pharmacy gains have come as Walgreens and other competitors have closed stores, raising questions about whether the growth can be sustained. The business also remains exposed to consumer pressure and competition from online pharmacies.

Caremark, meanwhile, faces continued regulatory scrutiny and potential headwinds from changes to the federal 340B drug discount program, which requires drugmakers to offer discounted medications to eligible hospitals and other healthcare providers serving low-income and vulnerable patients.

CVS participates in the program by providing pharmacy services to those organizations, but tweaks to manufacturer data-sharing policies and potential reimbursement changes could weigh on that business in 2027, Tanquilut said.

Aetna, meanwhile, is still working through a multiyear plan to rebuild its margins, as it looks to improve the profitability of its Medicare Advantage business.

Ultimately, CVS is betting that the value of letting investors assess its businesses separately will outweigh the benefits of keeping them under one roof. The split will test whether each can thrive independently.

#105. Second buyer makes rescue bid for Poundland as retailer faces collapse

A second buyer has made a rescue bid for Poundland as the discount retailer teeters on the brink of collapse, with thousands of jobs at risk.

London-based Xyra Group has offered to pay off half of Poundland's outstanding debts - a key sticking point in takeover talks so far - immediately. The rest would be deferred.

With Poundland close to collapse, US-based owners Gordon Brothers have already rejected a rival bid from an Irish property group which would have saved its 12,000 staff.

Gordon Brothers said the business has faced years of instability and "remains challenged and vulnerable".

Sources familiar with the offer said Xyra is offering to immediately pay off Â£12.5m of an outstanding Â£30m loan on Poundland's books.

The offer came as Business Secretary Jonathan Reynolds held crunch talks with Gordon Brothers about the future of the business, urging the investment firm to protect staff however possible.

Gordon Brothers, which bought Poundland for Â£1 last June, committed at the meeting to prioritise offers which protect its shops and workers.

The company said: "We are pursuing all avenues through the sale process to find a solution which protects as many jobs as possible and preserves value. Work continues at pace, engaging in active discussions on offers with interested parties."

Xyra is offering to pay off a higher proportion of Poundland's outstanding debts than Martin Property Group, which previously submitted a bid that Gordon Brothers rejected.

But doubts remain about the credibility of Xyra's offer, as there is little evidence they meet Gordon Brothers' tests, including whether they have the money and whether they would be good long-term owners of Poundland.

Sources pointed to the appointment of Andrew Brode to Xyra's board as a sign of its credibility. Brode works for RWS - a company that provides AI solutions for businesses.

Judith MacKenzie, head of Downing Fund Managers, told the BBC Brode is "well known and very credible" among small businesses in the UK.

But she said there is "hardly any information" publicly available about Xyra. "So, although some of the people look good, I can't find any evidence to say that they've got the money to back it up yet," MacKenzie added.

Gordon Brothers said: "Our intent throughout the comprehensive sale process has been to protect as many jobs as possible and preserve value in order to deliver the best available outcome for all the people, creditors and local communities who care about this business as much as we do."

Both Reynolds and Prime Minister Andy Burnham have questioned why a deal for the future of Poundland has not yet been made.

Though Xyra made an offer to take over the whole business, it is unclear whether it would keep Poundland together as its owner or break the company up.

Several Poundland suppliers have said they would also go bust if the retailer collapses. Its founder Steve Smith said two or three thousand suppliers to the firm, those who solely work for Poundland, would be caught up in the collapse.

He added that not only would Poundland's 12,000 staff be affected in that case, but potentially up to 30,000 overall.

#104. Voters rejected AI data centers over resource concerns. Here's why measuring water usage is hard

Voters made it clear this week that they want to see limits put on data centers for artificial intelligence, passing 40 of 41 proposed ballot measures that would place restrictions on some of the more than 2,000 new facilities planned for communities around the nation.

Water and power top the list of ratepayers' concerns. But calculating how much each data center draws from local resources is an elusive task, with operators typically reporting usage figures companywide instead of site by site.

Giant 1-gigawatt AI data centers from the likes of Meta, Google, Amazon and SpaceX can consume up to 10 million gallons of water per day, according to Shaolei Ren, a data center researcher and professor at University of California, Riverside.

The exact number varies greatly depending on what type of hardware is inside a data center, where it's built, and what method is used to cool the servers and the building as a whole.

The complex ways data centers use water have left room for misinformation from both the builders and those fighting against them. CNBC talked to experts to get a clearer picture.

Data centers need serious internal cooling methods to keep chips and servers from overheating.

Legacy data centers, filled with lower-power servers used for things like networking or storage, are often air-cooled. Large fans move hot air away from the servers and outside the building.

But modern AI data centers are filled with high-power, denser graphics processing units, or GPUs, which Ren said can run so hot that the servers can "boil your eggs."

Water is about 3,000 times more efficient for cooling down these GPUs, according to Equinix sustainability head Christopher Wellise.

GPUs "run at such a high wattage, a high temperature, you would need nearly hurricane-force winds to actually cool them down if you were just using air," Wellise told CNBC in an interview.

That's why the latest rack-scale AI systems, Nvidia's Vera Rubin and Advanced Micro Devices' Helios, rely on liquid cooling. Most large AI data centers use a closed-loop liquid cooling system that uses very little water on a daily basis.

At an Equinix data center in San Jose, California, CNBC got an up-close look at the closed-loop liquid cooling system used to keep temperatures down in a row of eight Nvidia Grace Blackwell racks owned by AI marketing startup Alembic.

Equinix builds colocation data centers, where companies like Alembic rent space to run their own server racks, while Equinix supplies the power, network connections, interconnectivity and cooling.

Like many data center operators, Equinix doesn't disclose water consumption at individual sites. In 2025, Equinix operations as a whole consumed 1.2 billion gallons of water, up 10% from a year prior.

Despite closed-loop internal cooling like what Equinix uses in San Jose, total water consumption is far more dependent on an additional cooling process that happens outside the data center.

"How do we reject the heat from the closed loop to the ambient environment?" Ren said. "That's where the water usage comes in."

In Lebanon, Indiana, for example, Meta is building a 1GW data center that it says uses a "closed-loop, liquid-cooled system that recirculates the same water and will use zero water for a majority of the year."

Water allocation for the site, however, could ramp up to 8 million gallons per day by 2031, according to the company's law firm. Meta says that figure represents peak usage on the hottest days. "We anticipate rarely reaching this maximum usage level, currently projected as only a few days per year," Meta told CNBC in a statement.

That's because the hottest days of the year will require more water for that external cooling process, removing the heat from that separate internal closed-loop system.

There are two primary methods for this external cooling process at a data center: dry cooling and evaporative cooling. While dry cooling uses far less water, it can require far more power, especially in hotter climates where many of these AI data centers are being built.

"There's no sort of silver bullet," Wellise said. "At some point you need to eject that heat out to the atmosphere. And that's where those trade-offs occur."

Dry cooling relies on fans to blow air across heat exchangers, using the ambient air to cool it down. Those fans are less effective when the outside temperature is high, so data centers turn on refrigeration units to cool down the air first.

"That's a very power-intensive process, and that's exactly why we have power shortages in the summer," Ren said. "Because everybody else turns on their AC."

Evaporative cooling, meanwhile, uses very little power. The hot water circulates through cooling towers or over wet pads that are exposed to air, and the portion that evaporates carries heat away with it. Large amounts of water can be lost through that evaporation on the hottest days.

In between these two extremes, hybrid methods are common as companies try to find a balance.

Some data centers, like Equinix's in San Jose, use dry cooling in the colder months and evaporative cooling when it's hot outside. In Indiana, Meta relies on dry cooling and keeps power consumption down in hotter months by spraying a mist to cool the air instead of relying solely on refrigeration units.

Near some data center projects, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft and others are building water treatment plants to help treat and recycle the necessary water. Meta, for example, spent $100 million on a new water and wastewater system near its data center in Kuna, Idaho.

Outside Memphis, Tennessee, Elon Musk pledged $80 million to build a water recycling plant that's meant to turn treated municipal wastewater into cooling water for SpaceX's Colossus data centers. The first data center there, Colossus 1, came online rapidly in 2024, but construction on the water treatment plant was put on hold in April of this year.

Colossus 1 has a water allocation of up to 1.28 million gallons per day, although it hasn't yet reached that threshold, according to the local utility, Memphis Light, Gas and Water.

Tech firms are also building their own power plants on some data center campuses in an effort to secure enough supply, but those also consume a large amount of water. Colossus gets energy from gas-powered turbines it installed on-site, which have been the subject of multiple lawsuits over alleged Clean Air Act violations and noise pollution.

Meanwhile, some chip companies are directly working on ways to bring the heat of a chip down so less cooling will be needed to begin with.

South Korean startup DeepX makes an AI processor it says runs up to 100°F cooler than competitors. Hyundai Motor will use the DeepX chip in robotics offerings.

SK Hynix, the world's leading maker of high-bandwidth memory, told CNBC in July that it's working on a low-heat memory technology called iHBM that can reduce thermal resistance by 30%.

At Equinix, Wellise said each new site undergoes a water risk analysis to determine which external cooling method to deploy in areas with scarce water.

"Communities are beginning to ask the right questions. They're concerned about water consumption," Wellise said. "We take resource consumption, resource use very seriously."

#102. E.l.f. Beauty posts 51% sales increase, raises full-year guidance

E.l.f. Beauty on Wednesday blew past Wall Street expectations as it posted a 51% increase in net sales in its second fiscal quarter.

The beauty company said it saw its 31st consecutive quarter of net sales growth, attributing the streak to strong performance across its portfolio. The revenue spike was driven by both retail and e-commerce channels across the U.S. and internationally, the company added.

"The thing I'm most pleased about is we saw strength across our entire portfolio," CEO Tarang Amin told CNBC in an interview. "Every one of our brands contributed strong growth this quarter."

Amin added that E.l.f. is the most-searched brand on TikTok Shop and the No. 2 brand in cosmetics on the platform. The company also said it saw strong results for Rhode, the skincare brand it acquired last year.

The company raised its full fiscal-year guidance following the better-than-anticipated quarter. It now expects net sales in the range of $2 billion to $2.03 billion, compared with a previous range of between $1.94 billion and $1.97 billion. It also upped its adjusted EBITDA projection to a range of $421 million to $427 million from between $401 million and $407 million.

E.l.f. said it now expects adjusted earnings per share for the year to come in between $3.82 and $3.86, exceeding prior guidance of between $3.50 and $3.55 per share. It's also expecting a net income range of $231 million to $233 million, up from a previous range of $212 million to $215 million.

It had already raised its guidance last quarter after outperforming its expectations.

Amin said the company is "very bullish" on the holiday season, expecting high sales through the end of the year.

Here's how the company performed in its second fiscal quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the period ended Sept. 30, E.l.f. reported net income of $30.03 million, or 50 cents per share, up significantly from roughly $3 million, or 5 cents per share, the year prior.

E.l.f. also saw significant growth in its international channels, with business up 165%. Amin told CNBC that only 25% of the company's business is outside of the U.S. and that it has a "tremendous opportunity" to build on that momentum.

The company also said its gross margin increased 1.6% during the quarter, including a 0.3% benefit from tariff refunds. Amin previously said the retailer planned to fully reinvest that money into pricing and marketing to continue driving growth.

Though E.l.f. received most of its refunds in its first fiscal quarter, some of those funds flowed through the second quarter, and the company expects to receive a yearly total of around $55 million. Amin told CNBC on Wednesday that the company lowered the price of about 10% of its units by $1 because of the tariff benefits.

"We're seeing great unit momentum based on that," he said.

#101. Drunk and dangerous e-bike riders face on-the-spot fines under crackdown

People riding drunk or dangerously on e-bikes â and those who jump red lights - could face on-the-spot fines under plans to curb anti-social behaviour.

Firms which run shared e-bike schemes will also be expected to manage their fleets better and provide designated parking areas to keep pavements safe.

They could also be required to take out insurance so people can claim compensation if they are hit by someone on a shared bike.

It's part of a crackdown on what ministers called the "wild west" of e-biking â with a patchwork of rules across the country often leaving the streets unsafe for pedestrians.

A consultation launched on Thursday sets out proposed national standards to make journeys safer for everyone and keep pavements clear, particularly for wheelchair users and families with prams.

The first e-bikes were launched in Exeter in 2016 and arrived in London a year later, growing to become a popular commuter option.

According to the Greater London Authority, 37 million trips were taken on dockless e-bikes last year â a third more than the previous year.

Shared e-bikes - such as Lime, Voi, Beryl, Dott, Forest and Bolt - make short journeys quick and convenient.

While some areas enforce rules that mean shared bikes can only be left in specific areas, others have looser rules.

Under the government's proposals, local leaders will be able to license schemes in their area, allowing them to enforce minimum standards and better integrate e-bikes into existing transport networks.

Measures could include requiring operators to have a parking plan and to manage fleet sizes and operating areas, so bikes aren't overcrowding streets.

The consultation will also consider ways to tackle antisocial and illegal behaviour, to prevent users from riding shared cycles drunk, underage, or on pavements. There will also be clear consequences set out â such as fines for this type of behaviour.

The government is also consulting on whether local leaders should have extra powers, including setting fees and requirements for operators to share data to improve enforcement and journey planning.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham said the e-bike clampdown was a "small but important everyday fix" which would make streets safer.

Transport Secretary Heidi Alexander said the changes would "stop nuisance parking, tackle antisocial behaviour and make journeys safer".

Bosses at Lime, Voi and Bolt all welcomed the proposals, adding that consistent standards being applied across the country would provide clarity and certainty for operators, riders and the general public.

#99. Chinese AI lab Moonshot turned to Europe for Nvidia Blackwell access amid U.S. China curbs, sources tell CNBC

Moonshot AI, a Chinese artificial intelligence lab that the White House accused of accessing Nvidia advanced chips remotely in East Asia, has sought access to the chips through data centers in Europe, sources have told CNBC.

Deals have been discussed this year for Moonshot to rent capacity from neoclouds hosting Nvidia graphics processing units, or GPUs, at facilities in Europe, four people familiar with the matter said. They asked not to be named because they were discussing private information.

Nvidia's effective monopoly over the most powerful chips has made U.S. export controls a key tool in Washington's effort to preserve its advantage over China in the AI race. American AI labs such as OpenAI and Anthropic are locked in a training and pricing battle with Chinese developers, including Moonshot AI, which makes the Kimi family of models.

Moonshot was looking to lease capacity powered by Nvidia's Blackwell systems, advanced chips currently subject to those export controls which effectively ban their import into China, three of the sources told CNBC.

In July, the White House said the Chinese AI lab had accessed Nvidia's Blackwell chips via a facility in Thailand.

U.S. export controls restrict the shipment of Nvidia's most advanced chips to China, but remote access via overseas data centers offers a loophole. Current law does not give regulators broad authority to control companies' access to those chips through cloud services hosted overseas.

Moonshot, Nvidia, the White House, the U.S. Department of Commerce, China's embassy in the U.K. and its Ministry of Foreign Affairs did not respond to a request for comment.

Chinese developers have made notable capability gains in the past six months and greater access to Nvidia chips could put even more pressure on their American rivals.

To lease Nvidia computing power in Europe, a Chinese AI company would typically rent capacity from a non-Chinese neocloud or data center operator that can purchase advanced chips.

Moonshot was interested in leasing capacity on clusters for inference, which refers to serving models to end users, in Europe, two of the sources said. One of the sources said they were also aware of interest in clusters for training.

Talks for arrangements that would see Moonshot rent access to clusters of 8,000 or more Blackwell GPUs hosted in the region have taken place, one source said. They described the Chinese AI lab as "very aggressive" in its pursuit of more computing power.

One of the sources told CNBC that talks on securing Moonshot compute in Europe had taken place since the White House said it had done so via Thailand. Moonshot did not comment at the time.

Founded in 2023, Beijing-based Moonshot AI is one of China's leading model builders. It released a new model in July that Moonshot said closed the gap with leading systems built by Anthropic and OpenAI.

The model saw significant uptake as a cheaper alternative to U.S. offerings. Chinese AI models have surged in global adoption in 2026, amid capability gains and lower costs than U.S. rivals.

Around the time Kimi K3 was released, Moonshot said demand had stretched its capacity, and it temporarily paused new subscriptions to prioritize compute for current members. "We're adding capacity as fast as we can and will reopen new subscription spots in batches," the company said in a LinkedIn post.

Leasing AI compute is a booming industry, with customers, or offtakers, typically taking capacity for a fee and paying an upfront cost to secure the deal, which is a percentage of the lifetime contract value.

One source said it had been proposed that Moonshot pay 30% to 50% more upfront than a non-Chinese offtaker would.

Arrangements with Chinese customers can be more lucrative upfront, as neoclouds and financing firms look to hedge against a potential future ban of remote access agreements.

In Washington, lawmakers have proposed the Remote Access Security Act (RASA) to expand U.S. export controls to include the cloud-based access of critical hardware and software. It passed the House of Representatives in January but has not passed the Senate.

The Financial Times reported on Tuesday that the chip giant told cloud providers selling access to their chips that they must sell at least 51% of services to non-Chinese customers.

Asked about the reports earlier this week, an Nvidia spokesperson said: "America cannot win the AI race unless America's AI infrastructure wins worldwide — if the world's clouds rely on foreign-made chips, the model and application layers will be foreign-built as well."

Nvidia said in August that Washington had licensed some sales of its H200 AI chips, an older generation, but that "such sales were restricted" by the Chinese government, which has sought to boost domestic chipmakers by limiting access to Nvidia chips.

#98. Three ways the interest rate rise could affect you

Interest rates have been raised for the first time in more than three years - and it could be good and bad news for you.

Here are three ways the increase to 4% from 3.75% could affect you.

Rate-setters at the Bank make changes to interest rates to fulfil their role of keeping price rises at a steady level.

Higher rates are meant to encourage people to spend less, reducing demand for goods and services and limiting price rises.

This impacts businesses in two ways - less money to spend among their customers may mean they sell less, but also the cost of borrowing to invest as a company goes up.

Many businesses, particularly small ones, have variable rate loans which are influenced by the Bank rate.

Firms say higher rates limit their ability to offer new jobs and wage rises.

Mitchell Barnes, founder and chief executive of Ryse 3D, which manufactures 3D parts for the defence and automotive sectors, said the "reality" of rising interest rates was to look at the cost of employees and people.

That could include greater use of robots rather than workers.

"It's going to cause issues with, I suppose, employment across all sectors for people," he said.

"You're going to see reduced employment, more automation, more robotics being put into facilities, just in order to increase efficiency and make that money go further."

The Bank rate - and where it is expected to go in future - has a significant impact on the interest rates customers are offered on savings products.

So, if you're saving up for something quite expensive, like a holiday, or very expensive, like a home, then a rate rise could be good news.

When interest rates were low throughout the 2010s, the returns for savers were poor, but borrowing costs were low too.

The answer then and now, according to experts, is to make sure you move your savings to benefit from the best possible rates.

What's happening to UK interest rates and what does it mean for mortgages?

You need Â£17,000 for a first home - here's how to save it

Faisal Islam: How high will interest rates go?

Leaving your savings in an old account (a product not offered to new customers), rather than shopping around, can mean you are penalised for your loyalty.

"Savers have endured years of unrewarded loyalty, with those leaving [a Â£20,000 cash pot] in closed accounts losing out on an extra Â£139 over the past three years compared to on-sale easy access accounts," said Rachel Springall, from financial information service Moneyfacts.

She also said locking away your money for longer, with a fixed-term deal, is usually more lucrative.

The other flipside is that renting while you save could get more expensive, because a rising Bank rate may well increase costs for landlords.

The big losers from a rate rise are those who borrow money, and specifically those on variable rate mortgages.

About 500,000 homeowners have a mortgage that "tracks" the Bank of England's rate.

Any rise in the Bank rate means an increase in the monthly repayments on their outstanding loan. For a typical loan, the cost is going up by about Â£31 a month now the Bank has raised the base rate, but it depends on the size of people's loans.

An additional 500,000 homeowners on standard variable rates (SVRs) must wait to see if their lender chooses to pass on any Bank rate rise. Typically, this would mean a Â£14 increase per month in repayments for a 0.25 percentage points rates rise.

If you, like 87% of mortgage-holders, have a fixed-rate deal, then the payments will stay the same until the end of your current deal, which is usually two or five years.

While monthly payments are not immediately affected by a rate change, future deals are. Lenders had already "priced in" the latest rate rise when quoting new deals. Expectations for the Bank rate into next year are crucial on what they offer next.

The average rate on fixed deals are much higher now than was predicted at the start of the year. It means those remortgaging, possibly after the post-Covid boom in the housing market, are facing the prospect of higher mortgage payments.

Some people have been choosing to move onto tracker deals instead, taking a view - including some risk - of the Bank rate not moving towards a series of further hikes.

Do you have a mortgage, a credit card, a car finance agreement? How are interest rates affecting you?

#96. Faisal Islam: How high will interest rates go?

Interest rates have risen to 4% and the Bank of England has indicated there are more rises to come. The question is how many? There is a split on the rate-setting committee here.

Three of the nine voting members referred to "rises" plural. One member voted for a bigger increase, to 4.25%. Three referred to just one more including governor Andrew Bailey.

My read on this is that the expectation is for one more rise in February. Two and done, but it depends.

It is painful for those on variable mortgages, but should not be seen as the start of a cycle of hikes.

Predicting where interest rates will go is like throwing darts at a board that constantly moves around the wall, with expectations of how long the Iran War will last and its impact on the economy and prices lurching from day to day.

The Bank's forecasts indicate inflation is heading to just under 5% next spring as a typical household energy bill surges by 27% in January to Â£2,150 a year.

Why have they raised now? High and rising inflation risks a further cycle of self fulfilling expectations of rising prices. It hasn't been seen so far, however. The war has lasted for 9 months, and led to oil prices used in the forecast averaging $108 a barrel.

And yet on the day they have announced the rate rise, the oil price is actually 13% lower than that having fallen in the run up and after the US mid term elections, reflecting expectations or guesses that President Trump will now retreat or deescalate from the Iran war.

The other notable factor is that the UK economy is performing much better than forecasts and expectations. This is underlined by the Bank of England's latest forecasts.

Growth has been 0.6%, and 0.5% in the first two quarters of the year. It is heading for 0.7%, the Bank said, between July and September, before slowing in the three months to the end of the year. Unemployment has stabilised, actually falling a little.

Some of this has fed through into a better performing currency, with sterling at its highest level since the Brexit referendum, when measured against a basket of currencies. This can help rein in inflation by making imports cheaper.

So, this is an unusual type of uncertainty. Even the expected further rise, probably in February next year is conditional on the war continuing.

The Bank of England stopped waiting for President Trump's war to end today, but perhaps the Democrats' resounding victory in the US midterm elections will stop the inflationary wave? The Bank has thrown a tentative first dart, and there is probably another to come.

#95. How the midterms could fuel the K-shaped AI trade

The U.S. midterms could widen the AI divide: tighter data center rules may reward tech giants with capacity in hand and squeeze debt-funded companies trying to catch up.

Democratic victories in key gubernatorial races raised the prospect of tougher scrutiny for data center projects, adding to investor concerns about how quickly new capacity can be built and financed.

In Texas, Democratic governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has proposed a statewide moratorium on new data center permits, although projects already under construction would reportedly be exempt.

As the markets digested the results on Wednesday, Microsoft and Amazon each surged more than 6%, while AI infrastructure provider CoreWeave plunged around 10%. Chipmakers Micron and Nvidia fell 7% and 3.17%, respectively.

Differences in borrowing costs are playing a key role in the split, James Sullivan, managing director and co-head of global fundamental research at JPMorgan, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Thursday.

Sullivan said borrowing spreads have widened significantly for neocloud companies, leaving their cost of additional capital substantially higher than that of hyperscalers that benefit from more diversified customers and revenue streams.

"The fact that you're seeing this bifurcation in equity performance is largely driven by the view that existing capacity is significantly more valuable now because of incremental supply constraints," he said.

The midterm results could reinforce that view if they make new projects more vulnerable to permitting delays.

BMI warned that a sharp slowdown in data center construction and AI investment could produce a greater-than-expected decline in overall investment, The Business Times reported, citing the firm's post-midterm analysis.

The potential impact is uneven across the construction pipeline. Much of the data center capacity expected to come online in 2027 is already under construction, limiting the near-term impact of new restrictions.

Projects planned for 2028 face greater exposure to permitting delays and regulatory uncertainty, investment bank Nomura said in a note Wednesday about the midterm results.

Tighter regulations on data center construction could become a more significant headwind to AI investment from 2028, the firm said.

Shares of Nvidia-backed neocloud CoreWeave, Nebius and Iren fell at least 30% in October, as tightening credit markets raised questions about how AI infrastructure projects would be financed. CoreWeave's five-year credit default swap spread has surged past 1,100 basis points, reflecting rising costs for investors seeking protection against a potential default.

The distinction may be how well companies can absorb delays and fund the capacity they do build. Hyperscalers have broader cloud businesses and more internally generated cash flow, while lower-rated neoclouds face greater exposure to borrowing costs, energy prices and refinancing conditions, JPMorgan Asset Management said in an Oct. 20 research note.

The asset manager said financially stronger hyperscalers could also secure cheaper capacity or acquire assets if weaker operators scale back.

#94. Consumers are spending on physical gifts even as travel tops wish lists, Visa survey finds

Consumers are hoping for more travel and experiences this holiday season, but physical gifts continue to win the lion's share of their wallets, according to a new report from Visa on Thursday.

The report found that more than half of survey respondents are putting travel and live events at the top of their wish lists. Still, only 15% of those respondents are prioritizing spending on experiences for others, while 27% of them plan to favor physical gifts.

That gap between what shoppers want and what they plan to buy is largely due to higher travel costs, lower consumer sentiment and an emphasis on value, according to Michael Brown, Visa's principal U.S. economist.

"There is some interesting trade-offs there that we're seeing on the part of consumers," Brown told CNBC. "If I'm traveling more, I'm probably going to be gifting those physical goods less, so this is really the broader context."

The survey was conducted among roughly 4,660 U.S. adults ages 18 and older, designed to be representative of the broader population of consumers.

Brown said budget pressure also factors into traveling for the holidays, as Visa expects more people to celebrate the holidays "a little bit closer to home" than in the past given rising airfares and general inflation. In September, consumer prices rose 3.7% annually, driven higher by surging energy costs.

Airlines have hiked the price of plane tickets this year as their fuel costs have risen by billions of dollars due to the war in the Middle East. And even if fuel prices drop, executives have said fares are likely to stay high, with fares up more than 22% in September compared with a year earlier, according to the consumer price index.

Those trends mean consumers will likely be more intentional about where they're spending, especially as inflation drives up prices across the board.

"Consumers are aspirational. They want those experiences, they want to travel, they want to be out in the world," Brown said. "However, the economic reality is with prices rising, with economic uncertainty, they need to balance the reality of today's economic environment with those aspirations."

The report also found that only households with an income above $200,000 spent more on trips departing between Nov. 1 and early next year compared with 2025 into 2026. On the other hand, many other income groups pulled back on their travel spending.

"If you think about that segment, a lot of them own equities, and the equity market runup over the last several months has created a lot of positivity among that specific affluent demographic, and I think that's one of the factors that continues to motivate even aggregate macro spend today," Brown said.

Meanwhile, less affluent shoppers are placing an emphasis on value. Visa's consumer survey from October showed that roughly one-third of consumers with an income below $100,000 are going to switch to lower-cost brands or stores when shopping for holiday gifts this season.

Though Visa is projecting a 4.5% year-over-year increase in holiday sales growth, much of that figure is due to inflation. Without those price increases, sales are expected to rise just 1.1% this year, down from the 1.3% increase during the 2025 holiday season.

Ultimately, Brown said the survey findings may be encouraging for the broader retail scene this holiday season. With less emphasis on travel and more projected spending on physical gifts, it could boost the sector at a time when spending is under pressure.

At the same time, he said that consumers may just be expecting to spend more this holiday season because they've become accustomed to the higher prices.

"But, of course, consumers are internalizing higher prices of things, and I think that's why they're reporting higher anticipated spend this year," Brown said. "I think these numbers just need to be looked at through the lens of the inflation and particularly consumers' perception of where inflation is going to go. I think that weighed heavily on consumer psychology this season."

#92. Wendy's rejects $1.4 billion takeover proposal from Nelson Peltz’s Trian

Wendy's on Thursday said that it is rejecting a $1.4 billion takeover offer from a group of investors led by Nelson Peltz's Trian Fund Management.

The group offered $7.35 per share in cash on Oct. 28 to take the struggling burger chain private. In addition to Trian, it included investment firms BlueFive Capital and Sixth Street Partners, plus Flynn Group, one of Wendy's largest franchisees.

In February, Wendy's board formed a committee of independent directors to assess any potential bid from Trian in response to a regulatory filing from the firm. The committee found that the proposal "substantially undervalues" Wendy's and does not reflect potential gains from the chain's ongoing turnaround strategy, the company said in a press release on Thursday.

"Our confidence rests firmly in [CEO] Bob Wright's proven track record of seasoned industry leadership, including restaurant business turnarounds," Art Winkleblack, Wendy's board chair and a member of the independent committee, said in a statement.

Trian declined to comment on Wendy's decision.

To stave off a hostile takeover, Wendy's has adopted a shareholder rights plan — or poison pill — with a one-year duration, as of Wednesday, the company said. Wendy's added that the investor group led by Trian rejected an offer from Wendy's for a standstill agreement that would have paused additional share purchases, adding to Wendy's decision to use a poison pill.

The rejection is the latest in the long-running saga between Peltz and the burger chain, which dates back to an activist campaign he led more than two decades ago. A Peltz firm has even owned Wendy's before. In 2008, his company Triarc, which also owned Arby's, bought Wendy's in a $2.3 billion deal. It later sold Arby's, turning Wendy's into a standalone company again.

In 2024, Wendy's named Peltz as chairman emeritus after he spent 17 years on the company's board. Trian executive Peter May and Peltz's son, Bradley, still sit on Wendy's board.

Wendy's also reported its third-quarter results on Thursday. Its same-store sales declined for the seventh straight quarter. However, its quarterly earnings and revenue topped Wall Street's expectations.

Shares of Wendy's fell less than 1% in premarket trading. The takeover offer had lifted the stock, which has lost about 60% of its value over the last three years.

Wendy's is not the only restaurant chain that has found itself in the crosshairs of activist investors recently. Applebee's owner Dine Brands recently adopted a poison pill to block a takeover bid from investment firms Arkhouse Management and Cobblers Cove Capital.

#87. Here's what the Democratic election rout could mean for the U.S. economy

The Democratic midterm election sweep could result in changes in economic policies related to trade, spending priorities and artificial intelligence. But the biggest forces shaping the economy aren't going to change just because the party in power did.

Affordability pressures, heavy levels of government spending and growth that is dependent on the artificial-intelligence tailwind are likely to persist, economists say. And all of those factors pose risks to the economy.

"It's in a very kind of fragile place, and I don't know that it would take a lot to push the economy off the rails, and recession becomes a much greater risk," said Mark Zandi, chief economist at Moody's Analytics. "That isn't the case at the moment, but I don't think that's gone away."

Zandi said he had been worried before the election that the economy was under threat from inflation, an underwhelming labor market and geopolitical dangers, and remains concerned now.

Democrats romped to upset wins in not only House and Senate races but also governor and other state races. But economic and foreign policy is largely set in the executive branch, and President Donald Trump remains far apart from his Democratic adversaries, though he said Wednesday he anticipates working with them.

"I don't expect any change in economic policy. The Iran war, the tariffs and trade policy, the heavy-handed immigration policy, all are done under executive order. They're not legislatively driven," Zandi said.

"The president thought they were good policies before the election. I don't know why he'd change his mind after the election," he added. "So I think they will continue to be policies that restrain growth and add to inflationary pressures and interest rates."

Zandi sees the AI hyperscaler build-out as a key to economic growth, but also a source of potential peril.

"The risks are decidedly to the downside because the growth is so dependent on AI, and the AI growth effects are so dependent on the equity market, and the equity market is so dependent on the valuations," he said. "It just feels like kind of a very fragile place, and the labor market is not well functioning."

Local opposition to data centers was a fairly significant issue in the election, particularly for Democrats, according to CNBC polling, so that could be a further economic headwind, though the issue ranked below cost of living, corruption and several others.

The election happened amid a dichotomy in economic views.

Topline numbers such as gross domestic product, consumer spending and the unemployment rate tell the story of an economy growing briskly while consumers spend and most Americans who want jobs are at work. GDP accelerated at a 3.4% annualized pace in the third quarter while consumer spending grew 3.8%. The unemployment rate is at 4.2% while layoffs are around their lowest level since 1969.

However, rising prices have overshadowed those otherwise solid metrics.

The inflation rate in September as judged by the consumer price index was 3.7% — up sharply from 2.3% in April 2025 when Trump announced an aggressive round of so-called reciprocal tariffs against U.S. trading partners. The Iran war has only aggravated those conditions. CPI was at just 2.4% when the U.S. and Israel launched attacks on Iran in late February, a war that has caused gas prices to soar.

And wages aren't keeping up with rising costs. Wage growth when adjusted for inflation has turned negative, down 0.7% from a year ago in September, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics.

One area where the two sides of the political divide could come together is tariffs.

Congressional Democrats haven't been reflexively opposed to tariff efforts aimed at leveling the playing field. But the Trump tariffs have been criticized for their sweeping nature.

Gary Cohn, who served as director of the National Economic Council during Trump's first term, said Wednesday that he sees room for movement on trade issues.

"There's become a much more universal view on tariffs and what they're good for," Cohn said during a CNBC interview. "Protecting U.S. workers and protecting manufacturing, protecting jobs in the United States, specifically from China, more than anything, is good and has been good policy."

While Cohn noted that "shutting down the borders between Canada and Mexico and the United States has probably not been so good for policy," he thinks "there's a compromise that could be reached where we actually have tariffs put through the legislative system and they become law. They wouldn't become executive orders that can be rescinded the minute the next president walks off the podium."

Democrats would also likely look to roll back spending cuts to SNAP food stamp benefits and Medicaid that were approved by the Republican-controlled Congress under the One Big Beautiful Bill Act, Morgan Stanley analysts said in a client note. But other policies that Democrats oppose would be harder to reverse.

"We think policy uncertainty since the beginning of President Trump's second term has been tied to executive, not legislative, action," the firm said. "Policies on tariffs, geopolitics, trade, ... deregulation, and export controls are all likely to persist, meaning we should expect a certain level of policy uncertainty to continue."

High levels of government debt and budget deficits also are likely to persist.

The national debt currently stands at $40.5 trillion, up more than $4 trillion since Trump took office in January 2025. Net interest on the debt totaled $1.05 trillion in fiscal 2026, higher than spending on any other category except Social Security and Medicare.

The U.S. ended the year with a deficit just shy of $2 trillion, or about 6.1% of GDP. Morgan Stanley expects that level to persist even with some Democratic officials preaching fiscal discipline.

While there are concerns that the change in power could result in standoffs over the debt ceiling, "Democrats are likely to leverage [an impasse] to win policy concessions," Goldman Sachs chief economist Jan Hatzius said in a note.

"It is not yet clear what Democrats will seek in return, but limiting the war with Iran, tariff rollbacks, the reversal of spending cuts, and expanded health benefits appear to be high on the list of priorities," he said.

Despite the various headwinds and political uncertainty, most of Wall Street expects the growth in the economy to continue — if not without hiccups and lingering issues over the cost of living.

"My sense is that the economic expansion will continue to roll on, featuring above trend growth near 2½ percent," said Joseph Brusuelas, chief economist at RSM.

Brusuelas also sees the opportunity to "reset" the trade agreement between U.S., Mexico and Canada after a year and a half of tension from the Trump tariffs and reprisals.

"We only have a 15% probability of recession over the next 12 months," he said. "The outcome of the election was immaterial to that estimate."
